| 274 | [2601.19636 Who Said CVE?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.19636) | **4.8/10** | **3.3/10** | A sidelight study of the PR security-identification ecosystem; the artifacts reveal the time window was not executed, the dedup key lacks a security ID, and Figure 2's hand-written counts are wrong, so the precise 69.1% is unreliable |
| 275 | [2508.18933 VISION](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18933) | **4.8/10** | **7.7/10** | paired counterfactual can be used for pseudo-correlation robustness evaluation; public code, Figure 5, and CSV cannot rebuild the unique split, and Accuracy plus pair metrics do not close across multiple rows |
| 276 | [2606.14261 Security in a Workflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14261) | **4.7/10** | **4.8/10** | The role-based vulnerability workflow problem is reasonable; the body claims 25 CVE, but the public workbook's 44%/31%/19%/13% actually all come from 16 cases |
| 277 | [2512.04106 Retrieval-Augmented Few-Shot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04106) | **4.7/10** | **7.4/10** | The narrow conclusion that similar examples beat random examples holds; four-category all-positive tasks, no split/raw output, cannot prove real vulnerability detection |
| 278 | [2605.24171 PromptAudit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24171) | **4.7/10** | **8.1/10** | The prompt-sensitivity evaluation direction is correct; public data violates the cleaning claim, and fixed-seed self-consistency plus non-standard metrics distort rankings |
| 279 | [2607.14628 BitCoder / Routing Ceilings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14628) | **4.7/10** | **9.0/10** | Synthetic cheat-sheet saturation and real-CVE collapse is a good prior-risk study; -51.1pp/-58.3pp use cross-CWE proxy, Gemma has many timeouts, and the raw CSV cannot recompute the two v1 headlines |
| 280 | [2509.24698 LISA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24698) | **4.6/10** | **7.2/10** | Knowledge base and dedicated/general Agent scheduling are instructive; curated coverage, 4/12 real hits, and hindsight loss table cannot prove comprehensive advantage |
| 281 | [2607.12524 Code Provenance / OSINT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12524) | **4.6/10** | **5.0/10** | Source identification can serve as risk routing; 93% is affected by task leakage and corpus structural asymmetry, and regex security scores plus 77% repair are not security oracles |
| 282 | [2508.11599 CryptoScope](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11599) | **4.5/10** | **8.0/10** | The cryptographic dual-path RAG and 12k knowledge base direction is worth doing; the main metrics are text credibility, similarity, and LLM judge, not detection confusion matrix |
| 283 | [2602.23121 Traditional CNN Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23121) | **4.5/10** | **4.8/10** | A 2024 work uploaded late; Juliet mean AUPRC 0.66, and real Linux experiments do not report negative-class false positives |
| 284 | [2605.29901 Dissecting the Black Box](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29901) | **4.5/10** | **7.8/10** | Interpretability artifacts are rich; the body model and artifact disagree, 100%→6% comes from post-hoc correct samples, and L0 is again almost entirely explained by token length |
| 285 | [2607.21641 Tool-Guided Retrieval-Augmented Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21641) | **4.5/10** | **7.8/10** | The combination of compiler, CodeQL, KLEE, and repair memory is worth prototyping; the paper is still WIP, clean-rate is miscalculated, the main table has only two models, and there are no semantic results/ablation/artifacts |
| 286 | [2607.05772 VIC-RAGENT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05772) | **4.4/10** | **8.4/10 (method structure only)** | Commit multi-stage division of labor is borrowable; the public code uses test labels to identify TPs and retains RAG memory accordingly, the core 57%-66% F1 has direct label leakage |
| 287 | [2512.21250 CoTDeceptor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.21250) | **4.4/10** | **9.2/10** | lineage potential and adaptive multi-round obfuscation are high-value red-team directions; 500 samples have no per-example ledger, and 1--12 rollout directly conflicts with 31/39/46 |
| 288 | [2604.06633 Argus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06633) | **4.3/10** | **7.8/10** | Historical-vulnerability-driven sink expansion direction is borrowable; no ground truth, precision/recall, PoC, or per-item findings, and Table 2/4 directly conflict |
| 289 | [2510.10010 SLEAN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10010) | **4.3/10** | **7.6/10** | The independent-analysis, cross-review, and arbitration file skeleton is borrowable; contribution proportion and definitive fix are self-reported by the final LLM, without apply/build/test/security oracle |
| 290 | [2601.01320 ALPHA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.01320) | **4.3/10** | **7.7/10** | CWE hierarchy penalty and repeated-run consistency are instructive; the main table uses penalty sum rather than formula average, and structurally biases SAST comparison |
| 291 | [2605.04260 Lightweight Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04260) | **4.3/10** | **6.8/10** | Transparent cheap baseline positioning is restrained; identifier rename by definition should not change metrics-only input, yet the robustness table changes greatly |
| 292 | [2509.13023 Solidity Execution Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13023) | **4.3/10** | **8.1/10** | Slither candidates, LLM test templates, and Forge/Kontrol evidence chain are worth borrowing; the complete pipeline is not yet implemented, only 7 manual experiment categories and 3 case categories |
| 293 | [2512.22306 Beyond Single Bugs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.22306) | **4.2/10** | **5.8/10** | Multi-vulnerability density problem is worth studying; it is actually synthetic multi-label CWE classification without execution validation, and the 40,000 vs. about 16,000 scale arithmetic conflicts |
| 294 | [2603.00195 SkillFortify](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00195) | **4.2/10** | **7.5/10** | capability, lockfile, SBOM, and dependency graph are worth borrowing; soundness direction, same-source benchmark, and expired artifacts make the headline unreliable |
| 295 | [2601.02060 FPEval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02060) | **4.2/10** | **5.6/10** | The joint compile, test, and quality evaluation of functional code generation is a valid problem; the main table does not close, 9/10 tests can pass, and regex labels plus public artifacts systematically drift |
| 296 | [2605.03697 Tailored Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03697) | **4.2/10** | **7.0/10** | A dedicated prompt classifier on known-vulnerability types and candidate functions; directly and repeatedly uses evaluation misclassifications to tune the prompt, and Table II negative-class recall is 0.87 not the abstract's 0.85 |
| 297 | [2604.17750 SDLLMFuzz](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17750) | **4.2/10** | **7.2/10** | The concept of dynamic fuzzing, static crash triage, and LLM seed backfill is borrowable; experiments, charts, and artifacts are seriously non-closed, and the main numbers are not citable |
| 298 | [2511.06220 HYDRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.06220) | **4.1/10** | **6.2/10** | Post-patch re-review problem is worth doing; the body trains with 773 dimensions and tests with 768, making the same VAE interface unrunnable, and there is no zero-day or residual-vulnerability ground truth |
| 299 | [2602.02584 Constitutional SDD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02584) | **4.0/10** | **8.3/10** | Versioned security specifications are worth borrowing; n=1 empirical evidence and the public code contradict each other, and the 73% headline is unreliable |
| 300 | [2601.18847 MulVul](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.18847) | **4.0/10** | **8.8/10** | Router, dedicated Detector, retrieval, and prompt evolution architecture is strong; no artifacts and missing same-model evolution comparison, so the central contribution is not isolated |
| 301 | [2512.23480 Agentic Supply Chain Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23480) | **4.0/10** | **7.9/10** | The multi-Agent perception, disposition, validation, and ledger loop is worth borrowing; effects, latency, overhead, and 90% automatic mitigation all lack experimental units or run ledgers |
| 302 | [2308.10345 GPT-4 vs Snyk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.10345) | **3.9/10** | **4.4/10** | Has early exploratory and historical significance, but the experiments are insufficient to support "GPT-4 is four times stronger" |
| 303 | [2509.18790 IaC Security Smells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18790) | **3.9/10** | **6.7/10** | IaC semantic context is worth studying; 3,066/3,478, pair split, out-of-distribution ablation, answer-leakage-style LLM prompt, and several F1/table misalignments undermine the main conclusion |
| 304 | [2603.08520 SCAFFOLD-CEGIS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08520) | **3.9/10** | **9.4/10** | semantic anchor, four-layer gate, and failure absorption are a strong secure-editing state machine; the complete method tests only 96 chains, same-source Semgrep gate, and no artifacts |
| 305 | [2512.06248 CFCEval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.06248) | **3.8/10** | **5.5/10** | benchmark/metric code can be inspected; quantity, weight, test, and correlation protocols are inconsistent, and GPT labels plus Exact Match are not real repair rates |
| 306 | [2603.05689 SecureRAG-RTL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05689) | **3.8/10** | **6.8/10** | RTL summary and top-k CWE can help small models; 14 all-positive examples, GPT-5 participates in data/knowledge construction, and no precision/FPR or public artifacts |
| 307 | [2601.06177 AutoVulnPHP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06177) | **3.8/10** | **5.8/10** | Two-stage filtering, risk prior, and constraint validation are borrowable; 99.7%/99.5%/80.95% and 351 CVE have no auditable evidence, and the public code is not the paper's system |
| 308 | [2604.25711 MULTIVUL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25711) | **3.5/10** | **7.1/10** | Multimodal context fusion can serve as candidate features; DiverseVul OOD four F1 all lower than the all-positive 66.67% baseline, and split, raw prediction, and code are all missing |
| 309 | [2511.09879 Taught by the Flawed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.09879) | **3.3/10** | **6.0/10** | Training-data security filtering problem is important; each model actually has only 5 independent outputs, most are non-runnable, and the only clear security difference almost entirely comes from the SQL prompt |
| 310 | [2605.07737 Securing the Dark Matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07737) | **3.3/10**; core numbers are unreliable | **8.2/10 (method concept only)** | Semantic enhancement + security knowledge graph + graph model conceit is valuable; the impossible Wilcoxon, empty data package, and mock headline severely damage credibility |
| 311 | [2601.08691 LLMs in Code Vulnerability Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.08691) | **3.3/10** | **4.7/10** | Inputs contain CWE/CVE summaries, commit messages, and bug spans, and split is opaque; the abstract's "uniformly outperforms" is contradicted by its own Table 4/8, and patches are only text-similarity-tested |
| 312 | [2511.04023 AdaTaint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.04023) | **3.2/10**; core experiments are unreliable | **8.0/10 (direction only)** | Project-specific source/sink learning and alert filtering are worth doing; 2018 placeholder template, convention conflicts, missing denominator, and zero artifacts make the headline unverifiable |
| 313 | [2510.10321 VulnGraph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10321) | **3.2/10** | **6.8/10** | Graph semantic fusion and open artifacts are borrowable; 93.57% comes only from a 4,664-sample selection-biased subset, and 72.18% of positives contain the `vulnerab` label word |
| 314 | [2601.00254 RAG/SFT/Dual-Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.00254) | **3.2/10** | **4.3/10** | It is actually security-patch classification with commits/CVE/CWE; F1, three t-test groups, data split, and public implementation all fail to close |
| 315 | [2511.03995 Hybrid Fuzzing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.03995) | **2.8/10** | **8.5/10 (direction only)** | Semantic-feedback fuzzing is worth rebuilding; Table VII lacks the claimed mean±std, all three targets lose to LLAMAFUZZ, the claimed CVE does not currently exist, and there are no artifacts |
| 316 | [2508.11710 Cross-Language CodeBERT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11710) | **2.7/10** | **3.2/10** | Incomplete 6-page short paper; sample denominator, confusion matrix, and headline conflict, and the multi-model/validation loop is untested with no artifacts |
| 317 | [2512.19945 Multi-LLM Energy Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.19945) | **2.6/10** | **5.6/10 (triage concept only)** | descriptor multi-perspective risk triage can be auxiliary; no zero-day ground truth, proxy cyclic validation, ANOVA effect size roughly doubled, and no artifacts |
| 318 | [2607.21983 NFT Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21983) | **2.6/10** | **5.8/10** | Slicing and knowledge prompts change model label tendency; 437/450 is only the positive-label rate, with no real vulnerability labels or confusion matrix |
| 319 | [2509.15433 SAST-Genius](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15433) | **2.5/10** | **6.3/10** | SAST+LLM triage+PoC direction is reasonable; 100% recall is incompatible with a pure-Semgrep-filtering architecture, and the public repository cannot support the main table |
| 320 | [2605.04000 Rust RL + simulated fuzzing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04000) | **2.5/10** | **3.0/10** | The public results come exactly from a full 4,789 false-positive labels; PPO/87 MIR/cargo-fuzz are actually DQN/24 lexical features/string simulation, and label semantics are reversed |
| 321 | [2605.19668 SCARA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19668) | **2.4/10**; core experiments are unreliable | **8.9/10 (architecture vision only)** | operational-state, graded remedy, and CVA blueprint are strong; 4/6 root-cause elimination violates acceptance conditions, the public repository lacks 15 cases, and CVA is a placeholder implementation |
| 322 | [2511.15033 Benign/Malicious Packages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.15033) | **2.4/10** | **6.1/10** | Dynamic package-behavior classification task has real-world value; paper and notebook disagree on samples, models, preprocessing, and label construction |
| 323 | [2604.03860 LiquiLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03860) | **2.3/10** | **7.8/10** | Liquidity-semantic candidates and multi-round validation architecture can be researched; AI-completion data, mixed ablation budget, different denominators, 10 CVEs without IDs, and uncleaned templates |
| 324 | [2603.17974 Repository-Level Dataset Proposal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17974) | **2.2/10** | **8.5/10 (research blueprint only)** | CodeQL injection points, four-role construction, build/test invariant, and PoV are worth implementing; a two-page research plan with no data, experiments, or artifacts |
| 325 | [2512.18567 AI Code in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.18567) | **2.2/10** | **6.5/10** | The AI-code security ecosystem problem in the wild is important; the author attributor mixes era/task/repository into labels, real data has no AI ground truth, and all downstream trends need redo |
| 326 | [2509.16275 SecureFixAgent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.16275) | **1.9/10** | **5.8/10** | The Bandit→patch→revalidate concept can be kept; the public code lacks the paper's claimed rescanning, five-round validation, and AES, and the 74 samples also have training duplicates |
| 327 | [2603.09044 CogniCrypt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09044) | **1.8/10** | **6.8/10 (architecture vision only)** | The LLM-guided concolic-path-ranking problem is reasonable; the all-positive main table cannot produce the reported metrics, and the public repository is only a corrupted scaffold |
| 328 | [2509.09970 Embedded Firmware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09970) | **1.7/10** | **4.8/10** | The problem of generating firmware then security and real-time validation holds; core metrics lack denominators and raw results, public scripts cannot reproduce the main table, and the repository also leaked plaintext credentials |
| 329 | [2606.10945 Context-Based Adversarial Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10945) | **0.1/10** | **6.5/10 (research direction only)** | The adversarial-context evaluation problem is important; VGR/ASR, χ², transfer, attention, and the n=560 defense table cannot form one recoverable experiment ledger |

This ranking must be understood by breaking the problem apart:

- **Batch 28 has the hardest real-world impact**: PHOENIX; 26 maintainer confirmations and 20 merged patches are strong, but there is no complete finding manifest or raw runs.
- **Batch 28 should most change the Agent trust model**: ALIBI; natural-language context must be demoted to a low-trust hypothesis, and its 90%+ ASR must not be inherited as an unconditional general conclusion.
- **Batch 28 has the most worth dissecting public engineering**: SecureForge; the pipeline is complete, but a same-source static oracle and imprecise MH cannot be packaged as a verified decline in vulnerability rate.
- **Batch 28 best guides context compression**: RepBench; borrow the AST/CFG/PDG typed evidence lane, without treating the scope-confounded 83.2% as a pure representation gain.
- **Batch 28 best suits post-submission governance**: the AI refactoring PR study; incorporate refactoring into the diff-aware build/test/SAST gate, and finding churn cannot count as a real vulnerability rate.
- **Batch 28 has the most borrowable method but the weakest numbers**: SAFuzz; the risk-budget scheduling is worth redoing, and the current split, recall, and migration headline cannot be directly relied upon.

- **This batch has the strongest real-world directed-fuzzing evidence**: Staczzer; 41 vulnerabilities at 40 runs per configuration and 10 published CVEs are hard, but it gives the target location and has no public core artifacts.
- **This batch's repair evaluation is most worth designing after**: Kumushi; the automatic oracle almost cannot distinguish Kumushi from Codex, and only root-cause blind review of 305 plausible patches reveals the quality difference.
- **Project-level source/sink and cross-procedure semantics are most worth directly implementing**: SemTaint; methodological value is very high, but the test does not measure complete FP and OSS only deeply checks 10/102 packages.
- **The neuro-symbolic obligation separation is most worth borrowing**: LeanGuard; the LLM can only filter facts and cannot itself discharge obligations, but Lean proves only the hand-modeled abstraction after extraction.
- **The real-world Web multi-Agent run skeleton is most worth borrowing**: MAPTA; 104 XBOW trace/cost are reproducible, but the 19 real findings are not per-item publicly confirmed.
- **The most complete small artifact for model/prompt stability regression**: the WordPress real-world evaluation; 360 reports are complete, but 4 GT plus 1,660 unjudged findings cannot support a low-false-positive conclusion.
- **The most solid finding-level real-world penetration evaluation**: EthiBench; 108 expert ground truths, complete findings/matchings/results, and reproducible metrics are strong, but only 3 public targets exist, so one cannot assert generalization to all repositories.
- **The TEE-dedicated repair chain is most worth rebuilding**: TEERepair; the DSL, context binding, test validation, and 2 merged PRs are valuable, but in Table 4 two of the three baseline percentages are off by two orders of magnitude, and the 89-case core result also cannot be fully reproduced by current artifacts.
- **The most typical test-label leakage counterexample**: VIC-RAGENT; the multi-stage commit-review structure is borrowable, but the public code uses the test `label` to identify TPs and retains RAG memory accordingly, so the 57%–66% F1 cannot serve as leak-free capability evidence.
- **The most typical placeholder-artifact counterexample**: SCARA; the operational-state, graded remedy, and CVA blueprint are instructive, but the public CVA does not actually modify the binary or recompute the graph, and the paper's root-cause elimination and new-vulnerability judgment also violate its own acceptance conditions.
- **The highest overall content value**: QuartetFuzz; **the strongest end-to-end security Agent total evaluation**: CyberGym-E2E; **the strongest complete discovery—validation system**: Antiproof; **the strongest automated repair method with self-audit**: ContraFix; **the most credible wide-domain real experiment**: 2601.19239; **the strongest real on-chain detect/exploit/patch benchmark**: CyberChainBench.
- **The end-to-end target-vulnerability capability is most worth using as the overall acceptance line**: CyberGym-E2E; 920 tasks and the S1-S4 stages are strong, but S3 can fix another vulnerability, so target-vulnerability capability must be judged by S4 with hidden PoC.
- **The binary evidence-first detection architecture is most worth copying**: Veritas; first fix the witness-backed flow, then let the LLM construct claims, and finally adjudicate with runtime evidence; the 2 sampled FPs must not be written as a full 2 FPs.
- **The versioned repair-experience base is most worth copying**: EvoRepair; borrow experience distillation, quality reranking, and failure memory, without treating post-continual-learning Success@15 in the test set as a one-shot independent-sample score.
- **The source-audit hypothesis-validation protocol is most worth copying**: VulAgent; TriggerPlanner—AssumptionPruner—FinalValidator matters more than increasing the number of roles, and same-sample context ablation plus low-FPR calibration must be redone before deployment.
- **The CWE semantic layer is most worth independently reproducing**: VulReaD; the knowledge graph and reasoning supervision can be borrowed, but CWE ID hits cannot replace path evidence and causal faithfulness.
- **Suitable only as a source/sink adaptation direction checklist**: AdaTaint; the project-specific-rule problem is important, and the current version's denominators, ablations, project count, and artifacts cannot support any headline.
- **The strongest real reconstructable vulnerability asset**: ARVO; its full success rate is 6,138/8,921 = 68.8%, and the 81% in the random-100 comparison must be listed separately.
- **The smart-contract guard-generation experiments are the hardest**: FLAMES; the 4,835/5,000 compilable and the best 22/108 exploit-blocking are reproducible, but 44.52% semantic equivalence and the split-leakage boundary must be listed separately.
- **The project-semantic knowledge closed loop is most worth copying**: KNOWDIT; KG—specification—PoC—reflection is very complete, but 21/57/0 is the five-run union@5 and cannot be treated as single-run stable recall.
- **The specification-first audit method is most worth borrowing**: Code-Augur; borrow explicit security assumptions, assertions, and guided-fuzzing counterexamples, without mixing known recall, additional PoVs, and maintainer confirmations into one total.
- **The multilingual real-vulnerability repair benchmark is most worth adopting**: PATCHEVAL; the paper's original oracle and the current verified oracle must be frozen, and 23% vs. 80%+ are not the same evaluation snapshot.
- **The repository-context secure-code benchmark is most worth extending**: RealSec-bench; 105 tasks and raw outputs are useful, but SecurePass is still bounded by the CodeQL + LLM security ground-truth ceiling.
- **The post-alert adjudication is most worth experimenting with**: LASAA; CC/LRE and trigger can serve as auxiliary evidence, but the 76 FormAI labels revised with output feedback are insufficient to prove industrial generalization.
- **The multi-vulnerability-density direction is worth rebuilding**: Beyond Single Bugs; the research problem holds, but the current 40,000/16,000 arithmetic conflict, same-source injection oracle, and lack of execution validation cannot support strong conclusions.
- **The repair experiment is most worth directly designing after**: Vul4Py; exploit pass, functional regression, human-semantic correctness, and cost must be reported together, not only security-only pass.
- **The cross-language benchmark artifacts are the most complete**: RustMizan; the compilable vulnerable/safe pair, context ladder, and public logs are strong, but 42 CVEs and single-run limits constrain model-ranking extrapolation.
- **The strategy-learning reproduction Agent is most worth borrowing method from**: Mastermind; borrow the strategy Curator and dual-build verifier, without treating the post-training Best-of-8 84.5% as one-shot capability.
- **The smart-contract PoC state differencing is most worth borrowing**: SmartPoC; Action-State DV is harder than "the script executes", but the 64 TPs are only manually sampled results.
- **The calibration-failure diagnosis is most worth borrowing**: CWE-Trace's DFI/HDD; the 84% contamination conclusion and the exact-CWE headline cannot be cited as reliable numbers.
- **JavaScript project-level robustness is most worth rebuilding**: SECJS; the research problem is important, but the three snapshots of paper, website, and code must be unified from scratch.
- **Best suited for adding a quality gate to all dynamic-validation artifacts**: QuartetFuzz; a harness that compiles and has coverage may still violate the API protocol and produce fake crashes.
- **Best suited for extending closed-source binary audit**: SLYP; real CVE/bounty confirms the direction, but 64/64 cannot be interpreted apart from post-run ground truth, best-of-run, and tool-permission differences.
- **Best suited for multi-layer AI vs. human secure-code comparison**: VULBENCH-CPP; the hardest conclusion is the sanitizer OR 2.15, not the raw ESBMC ratio.
- **Best suited for borrowing executable safety-property repair**: VulnResolver; 75% is SEC-bench verifier resolution and cannot be directly called the full semantic-repair rate.
- **Best suited for borrowing the business-logic invariant knowledge layer**: LogicScan; two evaluation setups — strict isolation and allowing historical-fix knowledge — should both be run.
- **Best suited for filling the post-crash precise-localization layer**: T2L; it solves trace-to-line, not open-ended vulnerability discovery.
- **Best suited for inspiring pure-audit evidence orchestration**: VulnAgent-R2; **best suited for copying the project-semantic knowledge closed loop**: KNOWDIT; **best suited for inspiring a complete discovery—validation platform**: Antiproof. None of the three should be trusted for absolute numbers before the core artifacts are reproduced.
- **Best suited for borrowing multi-Agent coordination, self-healing, and observability metrics**: SPEAR; copy the Planner/Executor/Repairer and same-logic centralized comparison, without citing the baseline-missing 94% PFIR or the single-case 3x speedup.
- **Best suited for studying a lightweight pre-filter**: VulnScout-C; the model route is worth reproducing, but the verifier must be blind to expected labels, with added real-project low-base-rate false-positive experiments.
- **Best suited for borrowing the natural-language-spec-to-graph-query interface**: CKG-LLM; borrow NL-to-SPARQL and deterministic execution, not the 74.9% F1 under denominator drift and cyclic SLM judge.
- **Best suited for proving "multi-Agent union turns recall into an alert flood"**: MultiVer; 85% FPR and below-random balanced accuracy matter more than 82.7% recall.
- **Best suited for security-spec governance and least suited for trusting compliance self-reports**: Constitutional SDD; the constitution/traceability can be productized, but 73% reduction and 15/15 implementation are contradicted by the public code.
- **Best suited for contrastive-runtime repair**: ContraFix; but 91.8% benchmark pass and 53.5% semantic-repair rate must be reported together.
- **Best suited for a real Web-vulnerability reproduction benchmark**: 2510.14700; the best Success@3 is only 22.5%, showing that environment, authentication, and trigger remain hard bottlenecks.
- **Best suited for batch-converting CVE into executable reproduction tasks**: CVE-GENIE; 428 successful reproductions are strong, but the LLM-generated verifier and a 50-case manual spot check cannot prove zero misjudgment across all samples.
- **Best suited for building a real-repository secure-code generation benchmark**: SecureVibeBench; C-SEC must be split into functional failure, PoV failure, and high-false-positive SAST suspicious items.
- **Best suited for inspiring a complete static-analysis—directed-fuzzing—sanitizer platform**: FuzzingBrain V2; the body's 41/26/23 real results take precedence over the abstract's 29/all-confirmed-fixes.
- **Best suited for distilling known vulnerabilities into security contracts**: Phoenix; it is contract synthesis over given vulnerable/fixed pairs, not open-ended zero-day detection.
- **Best suited for borrowing a production detection-rule factory**: RuleForge; borrow candidate iteration, synthetic tests, traffic enrichment, and the human gate, without treating IP reputation as exploit ground truth.
- **Best suited for borrowing a path-constraint intermediate representation**: VULSOLVER; borrow source/guard/sink and branch/context constraint, without citing 100% recall on three OWASP classes as general capability.
- **Best suited for borrowing a commit/diff-level security-review entry**: AgenticSCR; 17.5% is the comment-level composite correctness rate, not vulnerability-level recall.
- **Best suited for repository semantic graph and context**: VulWeaver; **best suited for a strong Planner + low-cost repository exploration**: DREA.
- **Best suited for general post-alert exploitability confirmation**: AXE; **the strongest smart-contract PoC/profit validation**: V2E; neither can replace upstream recall evaluation.
- **Best suited as a trigger-statement tool**: AutoTrace, but its headline metrics must be recomputed.
- **Best suited for borrowing smart-contract context splitting and independent counterexample filtering**: Heimdallr; absolute metrics need redoing after public core implementation and fair ground-truth handling.
- **Best suited for continuous governance of known-CVE duplicate vulnerabilities**: MAVM; 57.3% is the report-level correct-repair ratio, and real vulnerable-pair coverage is only 51/114.
- **Engineering implementation most worth borrowing**: codebadger, RustMizan, FLAMES, CodeCureAgent, Agent Audit, and OpenAnt; respectively suited for a CPG tool base, a cross-language benchmark, executable-invariant experiments, alert disposition, Agent/MCP-dedicated SAST, and an end-to-end funnel.
- **Best suited for solving the false-positive flood**: Sifting the Noise, but with TP retention as the safety gate.
- **Best suited for building training/evaluation data**: CVE-Factory; SecureCode is better as a data-governance counterexample and multi-round schema than as high-confidence ground truth.
- **Best suited for repair retrieval and a repository Curator**: RAVEN; only the architecture is borrowable, not the 83.13% that counts Partial Fix as success.
- **Best suited as safe-code-generation online-RL infrastructure**: SecCoderX; the learned vulnerability reward must be constrained by build/test/exploit oracle.
- **Best suited for building a vulnerability-injection data pipeline**: AVIATOR; 93% mixes bounded verification with function-local manual weakness judgment and cannot be extrapolated to 133 CWE.
- **Best suited for distilling known vulnerabilities into static rules**: QLCoder, but it must be clear this uses patch ground truth, not zero-day recall.
- **Most needing a CI threat model**: Contextual Bias and Words Speak Louder Than Code; PR description, commit message, author identity, prior analysis, and task framing should all be treated as untrusted input.
- **Best suited for extending deterministic differencing into a crypto-audit closed loop**: Chai; two wolfSSL CVEs are confirmed, but 159 downstream preliminary PoCs cannot be written as 100+ confirmed vulnerabilities.
- **Best suited for directly implementing a Node.js discovery—validation funnel**: LLMVD.js; borrow Finder, Judge, constraint extraction, PoC, and the human gate, without writing automatic side effects or the 36 Wild valid PoCs directly as confirmed vulnerabilities.
- **Best suited for cross-version exploit migration**: Diffploit; the engineering artifacts are strong, but before citing 84.2% one must disclose the conflict between the PDF line and 590, the headline 580, and the public 579+1 flaky.
- **Best suited for filling a repair Agent's input supply chain and four-layer oracle**: SWExploit and VulnRepairEval; the former treats issues as low-trust input, and the latter's PoC gate still needs functional regression and variant attacks.
- **Best suited as a negative baseline for naive cross-procedure context**: 2604.08417; it only proves that direct caller/callee usually does not help on known-vulnerability positives, and zero negatives make 0.98 F1 unrepresentative of real detection precision.
- **Best suited for filling out-of-time and vulnerability/patch paired evaluation**: From Lab to Reality; **best suited as a restrained continual-learning baseline**: Hybrid-CASR, whose core gain is only 0.016 Macro-F1.
- **Best suited as a dedicated security-reasoning base**: VulnLLM-R; public weights are usable, but the paper Agent, five-project raw runs, and 15 zero-day evidence are missing.
- **Best suited for borrowing cross-procedure context compression**: CPRVUL; the true same-model context gain is only 1.56-2.42 points, and the cross-model summary difference cannot substitute for ablation.
- **Best suited for filling specifications and trust boundaries**: SPECA, but do not cite its post-hoc top-4% human ranking as independent evidence.
- **Best suited for logical-evidence filtering**: EntailLLM; treat entailment only as an intermediate metric, not as vulnerability ground truth.
- **Best suited for constraining confidence and automatic release**: Security Calibration; one must look at False Trust, refusal rate, and execution evidence, not model tone.
- **Best suited for a causal-reasoning graph**: DAGVul; borrow the DAG and error taxonomy, without citing an 18.9% improvement before independent reproduction.
- **Best suited for alert-disposition engineering reproduction**: CodeCureAgent; interpret 96.8% as a plausible disposition rate, not a security-correct repair rate.
- **Only for severity grading after vulnerability confirmation**: ReVul-CoT; one cannot use 87.5% accuracy to prove vulnerability-discovery capability.
- **Most needing repair training redone with an execution oracle**: Vul-R2; exact match only proves reference-patch replication.
- **The highest independent-reproduction value**: 2606.04739 Revisiting Vul-RAG.
- **Best suited for robustness-evaluation extension**: 2512.16538, but executable semantic-equivalence validation must be added.
- **Most worth borrowing repair memory**: MemRepair; the experiments must redo cold-start and task-order control.
- **Most worth borrowing the budget-scheduling problem definition**: VCAO; borrow only the research problem, not its 2.7x result.
- **Best suited for proving "multiple LLMs do not equal safer"**: Multi-LLMSecCodeEval; its 99.06% can only be interpreted as passing a specific CodeQL convention.
- **Best suited for reproducing repository-level vulnerability-repair context engineering**: AgenticRepair; first fill fair budget, repeated runs, patch-integrity, and complete functional regression.
- **Best suited for borrowing multi-perspective counterexample roles**: ReasonVul; bind deductive, inductive, and abductive to different evidence sources, without treating debate consensus as independent ground truth.
- **Best suited as the exploit/PoC layer after candidates**: Co-RedTeam; the execution ablation is the hardest, but detection precision/recall is still insufficient for unattended operation.
- **Best suited for building a specification knowledge base**: VulInstruct; report raw confusion matrix and root-cause matching together, and cannot treat Lenient CORRECT as deployment precision.
- **Best suited for redoing a multi-role evaluation panel**: SecLens-R; weights, caps, and missing dimensions must be sensitivity-analyzed.
- **Most needing independent reproduction from scratch**: Securing the Dark Matter; the method concept is borrowable, but `p<0.01`, 14/15, and the 91.7% reduction are not citable.
- **Best suited for a multi-representation false-positive consistency gate**: Code Representation / FPR; one must truly evaluate source/AST AND, OR, abstain, and paired recall, not pass off the AST single-path result as a gate.
- **Best suited for adding an Agent-runtime dedicated rule layer**: CLAWAUDIT; the rule assets are directly inspectable, but the temporal split fails, so 75.1% can only be downgraded to fix-touched-file finding coverage.
- **Best suited for counterexample feedback after a repair Agent**: Bulkhead; borrow PoC, compile, SPIN counterexample, and functional harness, without citing the artifact-less real-vulnerability headline.
- **Best suited for filling the skill supply-chain data plane**: SkillFortify; borrow only capability, lockfile, ASBOM, and dependency graph, not formal safety, 100% precision, and 96.95% F1.
- **Best suited for borrowing the dedicated/general Agent scheduling skeleton**: LISA; the knowledge base must be time-frozen, and all new findings must enter counterexample and execution validation.
- **Best suited for reminding evaluation to keep the N/A main denominator**: QuiLL; abstain, malformed, and API failure are all deployment burdens and cannot be removed from the headline denominator.
- **This batch has the most complete public secure-generation benchmark artifacts**: HardSecBench; 924 tasks and 5,349 required files really exist, but retained statistics, Verilog coverage, and mutation oracle must be interpreted per the repository reproduction results.
- **Best suited for turning security-review prompts into a versioned expert registry**: iCodeReviewer; borrow the routing structure of 38 experts, without citing the convention-conflicting 63.98 and the denominator-less one-week 84% as general production effect.
- **Best suited for a targeted physical exam of the code-model supply chain**: CodeScan; borrow CWE probes, clean baseline, and multi-verifier, without extrapolating the about 0.98 F1 over three CWEs and 18 clean models into unknown backdoor scanning capability.
- **Best suited for borrowing Android static—dynamic validation orchestration**: A2; Ghera 47/60 can be kept, but 75 TP, 104 zero-day, 51/75 actionable, and 82% token reduction must be downgraded.
- **Best suited as a research blueprint for building a repository-level vulnerability-injection factory**: Repository-Level Dataset Proposal; borrow only CodeQL site selection, the four roles, build/test invariant, and PoV, and cannot be written as a released dataset.
- **Best suited as a paper—artifact consistency counterexample**: SecureFixAgent; the public pipeline lacks rescanning, the five-round loop, functional validation, and AES, so the 74-sample success rate cannot serve as repair evidence.
- **The four hard gates Batch 30 most needs to add to evaluation governance**: calibrate static findings by LOC/functional completeness/unparseable rate; LLM judge must not read ground truth and then act as the deployment verifier; strict isolation between the fusioner and test labels; the headline must mathematically close with the confusion matrix.
- **The three hard gates Batch 31 most needs**: the retrieval knowledge base must exclude same-CVE/project/patch clones; interpretive experiments must not manufacture a 100% baseline on post-hoc correct samples; semantic-preserving transformations must first pass feature-invariant tests.
- **The five hard gates Batch 32 most needs**: static/dynamic stages keep the full denominator; LLM properties must enter deterministic counterexample validation; few-shot and thresholds strictly isolated from the test set; Agent approval bound to an execution snapshot; similarity, static findings, and GPT labels must not masquerade as vulnerability or repair ground truth.
- **The six hard gates Batch 33 most needs**: plausible/correct/coverage counted separately; SZZ high-confidence errors listed separately; applicable recall not extrapolated to the full set; Exact Match and static alert suppression not called repair; CVE dedup not called false-positive reduction; paper tables and confusion matrix auto-closed from the same manifest.
- **The ten hard gates Batch 34 most needs**: functional/security/numeric patches on separate leaderboards; verifier calibrated first; gold-file scope listed separately; unknown not entering high-confidence reports; prompt transformations proving semantic equivalence first; ablation must retrain; LLM baseline not reading the answer role; positive-label rate not called detection; all tables generated from the same source; statistics must be traceable back to raw counts.
- **The ten hard gates Batch 35 most needs**: target-conditioned vs. open localization on separate leaderboards; released data verified line-by-line for cleaning; prompts checked for schema/content; package-registry oracle fixed to a snapshot; no-code/error not counted as security; conditional rates satisfying mathematical invariants; prompt evolution with same-model/equal-budget comparison; label rules not directly copied into features; LLM judge calibrated first; all failures keeping the full denominator.
- **Batch 36 has the most solid experiments**: KLEECopilot; the increment truly attributable to the method is the 6 independent locations beyond random-path, and all 87 locations must not be written as 87 new vulnerabilities.
- **Batch 36 best directly inspires the audit Agent**: Sailor; the division of CodeQL specifications, static candidates, LLM priority, and KLEE/ASan is the most complete, but the 379 locations still lack per-item public evidence and total cost is about 2.29B tokens.
- **Batch 36 best fills the repository-localization layer**: Antares; explore-first and the localization-dedicated small model are worth implementing, and VLoc Bench's duplicates, patch-file ground truth, and unpublished 3B must be governed first.
- **Batch 36 has intellectual value far exceeding experimental credibility**: GRASP and SCAFFOLD-CEGIS; borrow the security-practice DAG and the semantic-anchor/gate state machine respectively, without inheriting their Security Rate, DR/SMR, or latent-security headline.
- **Batch 36's most typical zero-cost-baseline counterexample**: MULTIVUL; all four DiverseVul OOD F1 are below the all-positive 66.67%, so "better than fine-tuning" cannot be written as true OOD generalization.
- **The ten hard gates Batch 36 most needs**: symbolic-execution increment counted relative to a strong baseline; crash/location/vulnerability graded; finding manifest and reproducer released together; root-cause dedup; the localization set audited for GHSA/task/commit near-duplicates; patch-touched file not masquerading as human root cause; invalid outputs kept in the main denominator; security-editing gate separated from the evaluation oracle; all classifiers first passing a trivial baseline; percentage points not written as percentage improvement.
- **Batch 37 has the strongest public benchmark artifacts**: SecCodeBench-V2; 98 cases, 90 dual-single-test tasks, Docker, and per-round results really exist, but the paper has no frozen model main-experiment leaderboard and 8 cases still contain an LLM judge.
- **Batch 37 has the strongest software-supply-chain scale**: TPL-Benchmark; 6,055 apps and a ten-tool evaluation are valuable, but the 946-app candidate set directly contains ground truth and the public package lacks APK/AAR/JAR and a unified evaluator.
- **Batch 37 best adds adaptive red-teaming to the Agent**: CoTDeceptor; borrow lineage/reflection/transfer regression, without inheriting the 500-sample, 14/15, and 1--12 rollout headline.
- **Batch 37's most typical positive-only counterexample**: SecureRAG-RTL; the 14-vulnerability design can only measure positive recall and cannot report precision/FPR or production accuracy.
- **The twelve hard gates Batch 37 most needs**: PDF original page takes precedence; OCR/paper errors separated; target-function vs. open-audit on separate leaderboards; candidate vs. open-world separate; must have security negatives; semantic-preserving execution validation; same-budget ablation; invalid full denominator; live artifacts frozen; CVE verifiable; per-round ledger; strong-model preprocessing cost counted.
- **Batch 38 has the strongest method and trigger experiments**: DIG; 138 Magma CVEs, 80 triggers, 18-vulnerability five-time repetition, and the 18/13/7 ablation jointly support the complementarity of generator evolution and directed mutation, but the 6 new vulnerabilities still lack per-item evidence.
- **Batch 38 best directly inspires the PoC Agent**: PAGENT; the combination of static rules, program analysis, coverage/profiler, and execution feedback is complete, and the final success gate must be upgraded from non-zero exit to root-cause alignment.
- **Batch 38 best builds a repository-level coding-agent leaderboard**: A.S.E; keep the 120 repository tasks and the three-dimensional scoring framework, rewrite the `v_after < v_before` security definition, and freeze Docker digest/per-task outputs.
- **Batch 38 best builds a plugin/skill admission layer**: EduPluginBench; borrow P0-P4, clean FPR, record/runtime split evidence, and fail-closed, without extrapolating the 100% recall on same-spec mutants to natural defects.
- **Batch 38's most valuable data and negative results**: Bastet's two-layer DeFi taxonomy and BitCoder's prior-distribution drift; the former must release the paper's annotation snapshot and disagreement ledger, and the latter cannot write the cross-CWE proxy as a matched OOD decline.
- **The new OCR hard gate added in Batch 38**: text extraction only locates; A.S.E's denominator/sum, EduPluginBench's product symbols, DIG's legend, and BitCoder's footnote all take the PDF original page as authoritative, and the paper's own conflicts must not be laundered away with "OCR was wrong".
- **Batch 39 has the strongest real data and governance evidence**: ClawHub; 67,453 skills prove that three scanner-class overlaps are extremely low, but the registry verdict is an automatic silver label and cannot be used to compute real precision/recall.
- **Batch 39 best directly inspires the audit Agent**: VulAgentRL; borrow CPG node/path evidence, structured reward, and trajectory replay, without treating teacher-node IDs as human root-cause truth, and give all baselines the same tool permissions.
- **Batch 39 most worth adding the NL/PL boundary analysis**: PRISM; the placeholder-to-output summary is novel, but the three Good-Turing sentences contradict each other, 353 pairs have file clustering, and the anonymous artifacts can currently only be labeled "temporarily inaccessible".
- **Batch 39 has the highest engineering verifiability**: CWEEP; the PDF, fixed commits, and workbook formula can close the 0.608/0.515 overall precision, cannot extrapolate recall, and auto-fix must also be separately validated.
- **Batch 39's method packaging most needs downgrading**: ORACAL and Tool-Guided Repair; the former's attack changes semantics and the causal branch comes from the authors' prior, while the latter remains an ablation-less, raw-output-less, clean-rate-miscalculated WIP.
- **Batch 39 OCR recheck conclusion**: for VulAgentRL's reward formula, ORACAL's four loss terms, PRISM's Good-Turing, CWEEP's tables, and Tool Repair's main table, the PDF original pages were all reviewed; the above contradictions are the paper's own, not OCR.
- **Least suitable for treating the core conclusion as a paper basis**: 2308.10345; 2508.11710 similarly cannot serve as core evidence.

- **Batch 40 has the strongest method and open artifacts**: OpenVul; pair-aware post-training and fine-grained root-cause reward are valuable, but the judge reads CVE/patch, the project is not isolated, and P-Pass is also not deployment precision.
- **Batch 40's on-chain investigation Agent is most worth borrowing**: TraceLLM; trace-first path ranking, `k`-hop budget, and decompiled semantics form a real evidence chain, but the baseline task boundary and the 121-expert protocol still need hardening.
- **Batch 40's public benchmark is most worth dissecting**: Reentrancy Detection; the dual dataset and raw outputs are valuable, and also expose 432/436 drift, missing-line-not-counted-error, and weighted/binary F1 mixing.
- **Batch 40's architecture is borrowable but experiments need redo**: MALF; TCPR closes, but ETN, entropy, coverage, error-trigger rate, repeated statistics, and zero-day evidence are all inadequate.
- **Batch 40's most typical data-shortcut counterexample**: VulnGraph and AI Code in the Wild; the former is polluted by selective embedding missing and label-word contamination, and the latter lacks real AI author-identity ground truth.
- **Batch 40 OCR recheck conclusion**: all six papers' formulas, percent signs, tables, and legends were reviewed against the PDF original pages; `149/65.77%` is a TraceLLM legacy source annotation and `0.881.00` is linear concatenation in the reentrancy table, and the core deductions all come from the paper/artifacts themselves rather than OCR.

- **Batch 41's method is most worth reproducing**: PBFuzz; the true value is separating LLM constraint understanding from high-throughput property-based search, and `25.6x` applies only to the descriptive median over co-success targets, not a complete paired survival analysis.
- **Batch 41's large-scale engineering is most worth dissecting**: FuzzingBrain CRS; the public service, scheduling, program analysis, and 23 strategies are valuable, and patch validation silently crashing, log loss, and configuration failures should also enter the design checklist.
- **Batch 41's graph representation is most novel**: Esim; SSG can enter a candidate-similarity retrieval layer, but project/source-family/clone/time isolation must be added, with similarity, same-source, inherited, and exploitable vulnerabilities graded.
- **Batch 41's supply-chain direction is most practical**: npm Code Slicing; keep the CPG-slice route, but an empty slice cannot be judged benign, cost must aggregate all slices per package, and the evaluator must first pass TP/TN/FP/FN unit tests.
- **Batch 41's statistics least citable directly**: BugSweeper and Embedded Firmware; the former's p-values are copied in blocks and coverage is selected on the test set, while the latter's entire main table lacks experimental units, denominators, and raw output.
- **Batch 41 OCR recheck conclusion**: 89 pages were all rendered and verified; PBFuzz `5214k`, BugSweeper's duplicated p-values, npm Table 5's 96.23 column position, Esim's 93.3% non-closure, and the firmware's 37.3% relative gain are all original-page or artifact issues, not OCR misreads.

- **Batch 42's evaluation framework is most worth borrowing**: Re-Evaluating EVMBench; keep cross-scaffold, Detect/Exploit separate leaderboards, and grader adversarial testing, but event dates must be automatically verified by a provenance gate and failed runs uniformly enter the full denominator.
- **Batch 42's safe-execution architecture is most worth borrowing**: STELP; the AST policy, tool proxy, and feedback loop can serve as an extra defense line, but cannot replace containers, low privilege, network isolation, and syscall limits.
- **Batch 42's multi-model decision making is most worth borrowing**: DVDR-LLM; use disagreement to trigger evidence gathering and threshold calibration, without treating majority vote, length, complexity, or CodeBLEU as security ground truth.
- **Batch 42's artifact recomputation is most cautionary**: Who Said CVE?; 7,622/7,621/7,737 are recoverable, but the time window, dedup key, and hand-written graph arrays push 69.1% to 71%-75% under different conventions.
- **Batch 42's abstract least citable directly**: FPEval's reward hacking and the vulnerability-analysis PoC's uniformly outperforms; the former lacks a causal experiment and its pass/clean implementation drifts, while the latter has several self-contradictions.
- **Batch 42 OCR recheck conclusion**: 78 pages were all rendered; the incident dates, Table 8 denominator, DVDR few-shot, STELP 0.981, FPEval 14.15/14.5, CVE 7622/7621, and PoC counterexamples are all original-page or artifact issues, not OCR misreads.

- **Batch 43's Agent method is most worth borrowing**: EvoVuln; compile security knowledge into versioned executable policies, save traces, cluster failures, batch-evolve, and use a regression gate to decide upgrade or rollback.
- **Batch 43's malicious-package system is most worth dissecting**: ProfMalPlus; the static slicing, dynamic behavior, and cross-component evidence chain are worth borrowing, but unknown-package execution must first fix host network, root, RW mount, resource limits, and shell-injection risks.
- **Batch 43's data asset is most worth keeping**: GiAnt; 7,711 real audit findings can serve as a knowledge base and severity corpus, and the current binary benchmark loses to the all-positive baseline, so clean-negative evaluation must be redone.
- **Batch 43's historical-knowledge route is most worth borrowing**: DeFiScreener; keep only "historical-pattern candidate recall + call-graph validation", rejecting in-repository self-matching, ground-truth Top-K, test-set tuning, and mixed-statistical-unit precision.
- **Batch 43's most important repair-flow warning**: Verify Before You Fix and Code Provenance; detector flags or regex disappearance cannot represent security repair, and PoC, compile, full tests, regression, and independent confirmation must be added.
- **Batch 43 OCR recheck conclusion**: 137 pages were all visually verified; ProfMalPlus 16.5%/9.3%, EvoVuln 71%/$50.06, DeFi 58/64/70, GiAnt 386/400, Verify 23.42%, and OSINT 89%/93% are all paper-convention or original-page issues, not OCR misreads.

- **Batch 44 has the most complete method system**: VIPER-MCP; keep the static anchor, dual mutator, runtime oracle, and graded validator, refusing automatic installation, building, and execution of untrusted repositories on the host.
- **Batch 44's cross-repository route is most worth borrowing**: INFRASCOPE; reference-vulnerability semantics and module routing are strong, but the candidate universe must be independent of the compared tools, and per-item status must be bound to fix commits and maintainer confirmation.
- **Batch 44's benchmark structure is most worth copying**: VulnGym; EP-Trace-CO and oracle subtasks should be kept, while adding open-world tasks, end-to-end precision, public split, and a complete evaluator.
- **Batch 44 has the strongest real-world corroboration**: TitanCA; 118 CVEs are checkable, 203 cannot be. Production discovery must maintain a per-item ledger of report, confirmation, fix, CVE, and public status.
- **Batch 44's false-positive governance is best for deployment**: ZeroFalse; group by project/CVE, fail-closed on API/model failures, and report the number of hidden real vulnerabilities together with CodeQL's original end-to-end recall.
- **Batch 44's neuro-symbolic boundary**: NeuroSCA and Rust+KLEE both show the LLM can only provide heuristics or generate candidate artifacts; SMT/KLEE errors, wrapper compilation, and graph databases cannot automatically prove the original program's vulnerability.
- **Batch 44 OCR recheck conclusion**: 103 pages were all visually verified; 278/56/46, VIPER v1's 4.6%, INFRASCOPE 131, TitanCA 203, ZeroFalse 64/67, NeuroSCA 122, and Rust 1,206 are all the papers' original-page conventions, not OCR misreads.

- **Batch 45 has the highest experimental-template value**: SEC-bench Pro; keep three-mirror, target attribution, and human adjudication, but the benchmark must distinguish target-conditioned PoC from open-world discovery and automatically check the candidate ledger.
- **Batch 45's formalization architecture is most worth borrowing**: KVerus; the dependency graph, project lemmas, and verifier diagnostics are transferable to security-invariant/guard learning, but single success, best-of-k, and maintenance results must be reported separately.
- **Batch 45's paired-benchmark warning**: Semantic Trap; the vulnerable-patched design is valuable, but first pass the all-positive baseline, then do project/time/clone isolation and executable semantic-equivalence validation.
- **Batch 45's engineering implementation is most worth borrowing**: HarnessAgent; the active-retrieval, error-routing, short-time gate, and long-time coverage layering, and Crash, coverage, root cause, maintainer confirmation, and CVE must not be conflated.
- **Batch 45's supply-chain governance**: Agent Skills should publish a frozen corpus, sampling weights, confusion matrix, and dynamic logs; Wilson, Rogan-Gladen, IPW, and OR must be automatically generated from the same raw counts.
- **Batch 45's strongest counterexamples**: AutoVulnPHP, RAG/SFT/Dual-Agent, and Rust RL respectively expose headline-without-ledger CVE, metadata label leakage, full-training evaluation/label inversion, and simulated fuzzing masquerading as dynamic validation.
- **Batch 45 OCR recheck conclusion**: 8 PDFs, 114 pages total; 469/3486/3983, 43.2%, 87.69/81.46, 99.7%, and 0.652/.590/.746 all come from the original pages, not OCR misreads; the errors come from the papers' internal conventions or paper–artifact drift.

- **Batch 46's benchmark and engineering template is the strongest**: EVMbench; keep Detect/Patch/Exploit, containers, grader, oracle patch/exploit, and transaction replay, but Detect must add penalties for out-of-report false positives, CI clustered by repository, with public raw runs.
- **Batch 46's vulnerability-confirmation chain is most worth borrowing**: PoCo; known-vulnerability description to executable PoC, patch blocking, and human-semantic confirmation suit audit-alert validation, but target-conditioned PoC generation cannot be packaged as open-world discovery.
- **Batch 46's dynamic-validation route is the most complete**: FuzzySQL; grammar, semantic completion, coverage feedback, repair, persistent state, and replay can form an execution-validation layer, and Fixed, author-Verified, vendor confirmation, and CVE must be separated level by level.
- **Batch 46 repair and mobile engineering**: Reflexion only proves the same-ICD alert-rate decline, and Okara's TLS flow and ART TI attribution are worth borrowing; both need independent security oracles, functional regression, and closed denominators.
- **Batch 46 robustness and formalization directions**: VISION's paired counterfactual and RampoNN's reachability-guided falsification can be kept, but one must first freeze the split, prove semantic equivalence, publish error bounds, and save per-run random outputs.
- **Batch 46's strongest evaluation-leakage counterexample**: Tailored Prompts iterates directly over evaluation misclassifications to tune the prompt; any prompt, threshold, judge, or agent-policy adjustment must use only the training/development set, with the final test set sealed once.
- **Batch 46 OCR recheck conclusion**: 8 PDFs, 154 pages total; 14/19, 0.87, 1900.82%, the H=10 zero, 31+29, Random GUI, and the 80/10/10 conflict were all verified against PDF original pages, LaTeX, or public artifacts, not OCR misreads.

- **Batch 47 has the strongest dynamic-input engineering**: PILOT; path prompting, external compile/encoding tools, coverage feedback, and a traditional fuzzer can form a valid-input construction layer, but the ledger-less 51/41/33/3 cannot replace evidence.

- **Batch 47's execution-validation sketch**: 2509.13023 connects Slither, templated tests, and Forge/Kontrol, but `not reproduced within scenario` cannot be written as safe, and the complete system implementation and comparison experiments are still pending.
- **Batch 47's hardest external-evidence counterexample**: PILOT's representative YARA case shares the same function, the same underflow, and the same `yara -C` trigger as a 2020 public issue; Locus's VLC issue number/vulnerability type also has mislabels and ambiguities.
- **Batch 47 OCR recheck conclusion**: 8 PDFs, 131 pages total; 41.6x/21.21x, 88.46%, 92.31/87.92/88.85, CVE-2024-32030, the CoT p-value, and `not yet implemented` were all verified against the original pages, LaTeX, CSV, code, or external evidence, not OCR misreads.
