| VulnSage | Complex input/object constraints require a generate—execute—reflect—correct loop, and the system did participate in discovering a batch of real vulnerabilities | 672/678, 254/242, 22/23 caliber conflicts, Table 4 labels PoE as exploit, public artifacts cover only a small part; cannot rely on 146/73 and per-vulnerability cost as recomputed facts |
| QRS | LLM-synthesized project-specific CodeQL queries produce realistic incremental findings, and five new CVEs provide external evidence | 39/39 defines ground truth by QRS-positive, 14/3748 is only candidate-verdict disagreement; cannot rely on 100% detection, >99% production recall, or global superiority over SAST |
| EvoHunt | Audit procedures can be explicitly versioned, evolved from verified experience, and transferred to weaker models; procedural knowledge is an important capability independent of model weights | Before complete artifacts are released, cannot rely on 6×, exceeding commercial products, conditional-level FPR, or 28 confirmed disclosures as independently recomputed facts; the current judge and human review are also not independent security oracles |
| Strategic Heterogeneous Multi-Agent | Heterogeneous small-model verifiers can filter a portion of strong-model false positives, and parallel mainly provides latency gain | On Juliet all configs Recall 100%, best FPR 56.2%, loose CWE matching and zero-local-cost narrative are over-optimistic; cannot rely on it as a production detector |
| Argus | Historical vulnerability knowledge can propose project/framework-specific new sinks, worth studying as a hypothesis lane | No ground truth, PoC, maintainer confirmation, or artifacts, Table 2/4 directly conflict; cannot rely on 23 vulnerabilities, clearly outperforming CodeQL, or RAG sinks being validated |
| PMDetector | Static taint, constrained defense reasoning, attack simulation, and deterministic checker are a reasonable responsibility separation for detecting cross-contract economic vulnerabilities | The abstract's 88% precision/90% recall order, fully fair SOTA advantage, and $0.03 per-vulnerability cost cannot be directly relied on; denominator, time caliber, and artifacts unaligned |
| DataCentricFuzzJS | Historical crashes can be compressed into offline risk features, and a real Chromium issue shows feature-guided fuzzing direction has external value | With large-fold duplication, source-label coupling, and single campaign, cannot rely on 85%+ precision, <1% false alarm, or "stably faster crash discovery" |
| SLEAN | Isolated independent analysis, symmetric cross-review, final arbitration, and persisted provenance are a usable lightweight multi-model flow | 31.9% is not an execution-verified fix rate, contribution/convergence/arbitration ratios self-reported by the final LLM; cannot rely on AIB advantage, 83%-90% risk reduction, or 22 correct fixes |
| Hybrid Fuzzing | Adding structured behavior novelty beyond coverage is a fuzzing direction worth independently rebuilding | Cannot rely on ten repetitions, significance, bug yield, time-to-first-bug, or CVE-2023-29488; the original table, external records, and artifacts do not support them |
| Multi-LLM Energy Reasoning | Metadata/config descriptors for non-source assets can do low-confidence risk triage, and model disagreement plus cost aid human ranking | Cannot call it a zero-day detector, cannot treat r=.72 as real vulnerability validity, nor cite η²=.31, ω²=.28, or 30% variance |
| CogniCrypt | LLM-guided concolic path prioritization is a researchable problem, provided SAT, traces, classifiers, and security specs remain inspectably separated | All-positive data cannot produce the paper's Table 7; 97.5%, 0.993, 73.2%, formal soundness, PPO implementation, and reproducibility all unreliable |
| EthiBench | finding-level semantic candidate matching, maximum bipartite assignment, duplicate single-column, repeated/cumulative runs, and cost joint reporting is a more realistic Agent evaluation protocol than flag/task-completion; Claude Code three-run mean recomputes | Cannot extrapolate universal model ranking from 50 balanced matcher validations, 3 public web targets, and n=3 significance; living GT must be versioned and historical results replayed |
| TEERepair | Domain-DSL constraint on repair structure with the LLM only binding context is an effective responsibility separation; 78/89 arithmetic holds, and 2 real PRs merged by maintainers | The Prophet/VulMaster/SAN2PATCH percentages are off by two orders of magnitude, baselines not fully same-task and artifacts lack per-example data; cannot treat 87.6% as a production fix rate |
| Attention Distance | Semantic saliency can be additional guidance for directed fuzzing, and error attention produces clear opposite cases | With failed runs counted by N.A./success conditions, headline conflict, random function split, and inaccessible artifacts, cannot rely on 3.43×, 7.13×, or universal superiority over mainstream fuzzers |
| VulGNN | A 1.10M-parameter graph model is a cheap candidate prior, and its F1 18.17 on unseen-project split is its true capability magnitude | Cannot write the 0.96-point lead over CodeT5 Small as stably approaching LLMs, cannot derive end-to-end efficiency from parameter count, nor rely on generalization conclusions with unpublished split/checkpoint |
| VIC-RAGENT | Commit review should split structural change, submit intent, candidate vulnerabilities, reanalysis, and final filtering into independent evidence stages | Public code uses test labels to identify TP and clean RAG memory, main result is not a blind test; 57%-66% F1, Recent CVE generalization, and RAG gains all unreliable |
| SCARA | operational-state constraints, tiered remedies, and post-fix root-cause/behavior/replay/new-vulnerability gate are an industrial repair blueprint worth independently implementing | 4/6 root-cause removal and 1/6 new vulnerability violate its own acceptance conditions; no 15-case artifacts and CVA is placeholder, all performance, ablation, and 7.04× labor conclusions unreliable |
| Staczzer | LLM-predicted call stacks can replace over-approximate static distance as DGF guidance; 41 vulnerabilities × 40 runs and 10 published CVEs support directed acceleration and real value | Input gives target location, real discoveries concentrate on historical patch-location; without core artifacts cannot treat 2.13×–3.14×, all raw runs, or open-ended whole-repo capability as reproduced facts |
| Kumushi | Automatic PoC/test/variant oracle counts symptomatic patches as success; multi-evidence FoI and structured human root-cause review distinguish real patch quality of Kumushi and Codex | Cannot write 85.4% plausible as a complete semantic fix rate, nor treat 129/152, 119/153, and cost as independently recomputed without public patch/label artifacts |
| LeanGuard | Separating LLM fact filtering and deterministic obligation discharge stably improves F1 on the current five CWE classes; unclosed obligations should remain context-missing/pending verification | Lean only proves the extracted hand abstraction, not original C/C++; with per-CWE specialized specs, some single-run, and inaccessible artifacts, cannot claim universal formal vulnerability detection |
| SemTaint | package-specific source/sink, call-edge repair, and dependency flow summary recover 106/162 CodeQL known misses, showing LLM can fill project semantic gaps | Test does not check complete FP, three-run union, OSS only deeply checks 10/102 and four new vulnerabilities anonymous; cannot rely on production precision, low false positives, or universal npm coverage |
| MAPTA | The 104 XBOW tasks' 80/104, tokens, commands, and `$21.38` cost recompute from raw metrics/trace; Coordinator/Sandbox/Validator with enforced PoC is a reasonable architecture | 19 real findings no per-item public evidence, 10 only under CVE review; single CTF run and sanitized system cannot prove tri-role causal gain or real vulnerabilities confirmed |
| WordPress Real-World Web Evaluation | 360 reports over six models, five prompts, four plugins, three runs prove model/prompt/repeated-runs significantly affect target-vulnerability hits, with a 0/90 common blind spot | Only 4 GT, 1,660 findings not fully adjudicated; cannot derive precision, FPR, unknown-vulnerability capability, or universal model ranking from the 35%–63% detection rate |
| PyVul | Real Python package install/complete/alert/hit funnel, project-specific semantics, and versioned vulnerability data are a necessary basis for repository-level evaluation; manual sampling and tool-failure taxonomy are reliably valuable | Public commit/function denominator, unique commit and paper inconsistent, split has exact-code duplication and no project/CVE/clone metadata; cannot rely verbatim on exact scale or best F1 |
| TRACE | LLM can localize sensitive functions and recover uncompilable fragments, then FCG/CFG and guard rules produce inspectable evidence; public CSV supports its positive precision direction | Real data only verifies positives, cannot derive recall; intro baseline, modified-snippet discarding gate, and FP explanation conflict, cannot rely on exact advantages or semantic-preservation rate |
| SmartEval | Structured specs, obligations, compile gate, dimensional rubric, and public generation corpus are reusable assets; the per-item weighted formula basically holds on the 8,991 main records | Cannot rely on the exact 9,000 denominator, paper stddev/grade, A/E ablation labels, 30-case human agreement, or 79.4% Slither finding confirmation, nor write generated code as universally better than experts |
| Retrieval-Augmented Few-Shot | On this four-class vulnerability positive classification task, similar-retrieval 10/20-shot clearly beats zero-shot and this limited fine-tuning, showing example selection matters | Data excludes all clean functions, no denominator, split, seed, or artifacts; cannot rely on 74.05% F1 to prove real vulnerability detection, low false positives, low cost, or RAG universally beating fine-tuning |
| ALPHA | CWE ancestor/descendant/lateral error and repeated-run consistency should be auxiliary dimensions of security evaluation, needing a finer taxonomy beyond exact match | The main table actually uses a penalty sum rather than the formula mean; all-positive, forced LLM CWE guessing, and SAST non-reporting max penalty create structural bias; cannot cite absolute ALPHA scores or LLM>SAST ranking |
| SAST-Genius | SAST candidates, LLM context triage, PoC drafting, and human confirmation can compose a product funnel, but independent discovery must be listed separately | Semgrep 125-TP's pure filter cannot become 170 TP; repo lacks GT, raw findings, models, PoCs, labels, and timing, 100% recall, 89.5% precision, 70% PoC, and 91% time reduction all unreliable |
| Code Representation / FPR | Cross-language fine-tuning can produce catastrophic FPR while F1 looks stable; switching representation with the same weights significantly changes predictions, and multi-representation disagreement can be an uncertainty signal | 0.583 FPR is still not production-deployable; representation switching not strictly causally identified, and the paper does not fully evaluate the true AND-gate recall |
| Bulkhead | The counterexample feedback loop of PoC, compile, formal invariants, and functional harness significantly improves the secure and applicable ratio of modeled PaTra patches | No public artifacts, negatives, cost, or real-finding details, cannot rely on 0% FP, 9 new vulnerabilities, 3 CVEs, or open-ended discovery rate |
| CLAWAUDIT | Generic rules miss Agent-runtime-specific code forms; current recall-oriented rules have only ~12% precision on live HEAD, requiring semantic filtering and human triage | Public tests have 125/217 pre-dating the cutoff, and the 602->446 construction is not rebuildable; cannot rely on strict temporal generalization or write 75.1% as root-cause-level real recall |
| QuiLL | On 30 real-CVE snippets, multi-model verdicts and rationales are both unstable, and human re-review remains essential | The 240-run arithmetic, N/A denominator, RAG isolation, and reasoning judge all insufficient; cannot rely on exact model, prompt, or few-shot rankings |
| LISA | Knowledge base, specialized/general Agents, and staged merge are a reasonable smart-contract audit architecture; Size Meta Vault indeed hits 4/12 medium issues | No precision, raw findings, reproduction package, or blind replay; cannot rely on universally outperforming tools, strong generalization, or avoiding the $7.2M loss |
| SkillFortify | The Agent-skill supply chain needs capability diff, lockfile, SBOM, dependency graph, and CI gate; the tool runs fast on a self-built regression set | Soundness direction self-contradicts, same-source templates, late-supplied, and point estimate irreproducible; cannot rely on formal safety, 100% precision, or 96.95% F1 |
| Repository-Level Dataset Proposal | CodeQL selection, Planner/Implementer/Reviewer/Verifier, build/test invariants, PoV, and injection/detection co-evolution are a reasonable research route | It is only a two-page doctoral research plan, no completed dataset, samples, experiments, baselines, or code; cannot be written as proven scalable automatic repository injection |
| SecureFixAgent | Bandit findings can be structured and handed to a repair Agent, and re-scanning before/after patch with independent security/function validation | Public pipeline lacks rescan, five-round loop, function oracle, or AES; 2,168 data only 740 public, tests duplicated and labels incorrect, 81%–88% fix headline unreliable |
| 2308.10345 | LLMs can output richer explanations and fix suggestions than traditional scanners | "Found four-fold vulnerabilities", "low false positives", "fixes 90% of vulnerabilities" cannot be cited as strong evidence |
| RECEIPT | Isolation, role separation, PoC capability, and browser sentinel eliminate reward-hacking false confirmations in this experiment; 30 accepted findings all have replayable cross-role XSS | Cannot extrapolate 30/30 to universal zero false positive or high recall; role/deployment boundaries frozen by authors, single-model single-run and complete artifacts not public |
| SeedSmith | LLM-generated structured seeds unlock crashes a default corpus cannot reach within 24h; ARVO's 16 unique crashes and Magma's 5 one-shots support directional value | Cannot treat 11–14× as universal acceleration for all common targets; common-success targets only 1.46/1.71× and not significant, and input is pre-given sink |
| VULPO | Under the same ContextVul protocol, paired context-aware on-policy training clearly improves Qwen3-4B, and public model weights allow independent testing | The patch/CVE-aware reward and eval judge cannot prove independent root-cause reasoning; the 0.10-point lead over DeepSeek-V3.1 is not significant, and 31-pair OOD insufficient to prove generalization |
| FlowArk | On high-duplication Android data-flow workloads, context-aware knowledge reuse lowers LLM cost 13.59%–38.47% with Relative F1 roughly flat | Cannot claim improved absolute detection quality or that random apps all save 26.83%; 1,060 tasks/$100 is converted from total cost, and Relative F1 only covers the union finding pool |
| FIRMHIVE | Runtime recursive delegation does build deeper and wider Agent trees, and public code implements directory/file/function delegation skeleton and validation flow | Cannot write 1,802 alerts as confirmed vulnerabilities, the 71% table is missing 16 and is not overall precision; cost, sampling, depth, and public PKH implementation all have critical issues |
| Broken by Default | Missing security guards on these normal-development prompts is common, and dedicated integer/length SMT conditions find a class of counterexamples default SAST misses | Cannot treat 55.8% as a real vulnerability rate or call the 1,055 SAT a complete program-exploitability formal proof; only 7 runnable PoCs and artifacts currently 404 |
| PHOENIX / SBIR (`2607.00555`) | Project-specific historical defect knowledge can transfer to the current repo via code-anchored semantic bridging; 26 maintainer confirmations and 20 merged patches prove the loop has real value | 5 of 31 still unconfirmed by maintainers, and no complete manifest, prompts, SBIR, or raw runs; cannot rely on its precision, cost, or full 31/26/20 independent recomputation |
| ALIBI (`2607.24964`) | Natural-language context such as comments is genuinely a high-impact attack surface for project-level audit Agents, requiring provenance, trust separation, and code-only re-review | 90%+ ASR depends on attack-model synthesized vulnerabilities and GPT-5-mini judge; denominator and static-tool ratio conflict, cannot be a validated universal attack-success rate |
| SecureForge (`2605.08382`) | Searching model failure prompts, amplifying failure modes, and iterating the system prompt is an engineering-friendly secure generation/audit regression loop, with public pipeline extractable | Does not strictly satisfy MH proposal-nucleus conditions, Semgrep finding is not an executable vulnerability, same oracle participates in optimization and evaluation; cannot treat 12.7%→7.3% as real vulnerability-rate decline |
| RepBench (`2606.25356`) | Compact AST/CFG/PDG reduces tokens and improves given-function vulnerability reasoning; structured program evidence deserves an independent Agent context channel | Graph only contains target function, source contains larger file, 83.2% vs. 53.5% is not a pure representation causal effect; single-model single-result also cannot prove universal superiority |
| AI-Generated Python Refactoring PRs (`2605.21453`) | Real AI refactoring PRs introduce and remove many lint/SAST findings, proving diff-aware build/test/SAST governance is required after refactor | Pylint/Bandit finding churn is not a real vulnerability, workbook is corrupted and paper caliber conflicts; cannot use this to estimate AI refactoring's true security defect rate |
| SAFuzz (`2602.11209`) | Risk-aware scheduling and semantic harness can be a dynamic-validation budget-allocation direction, and timing data suggests adaptive scheduling may have cost value | Problem-family leakage, deliberately induced bugs, single-run, and recall conflict undermine the main experiment; AUC, 72.9%/81.9%, and transfer conclusions all need redo from scratch |
| SolAgent (`2601.23009`) | Forge test feedback and repo file-system context significantly improve Solidity file-level generated functional correctness; public result base recomputes main Pass@1 | Slither finding decline is not verified vulnerability decline, same-source oracle, common-file denominator, and Min-Vuln post-hoc selection cannot prove generated code safer than humans |
| IPv6 Periphery / HLEV (`2604.19487`) | In the selected high-activity IPv6 regions, periphery exposure and routing loops still have large measurable risk; HLEV layered confirmation method is reasonable | Cannot extrapolate as a global unbiased census, cannot map banner/CVE as exploitable vulnerability, nor infer universal LLM exposure from a single Ollama |
| Secret Stealing (`2604.27426`) | Malicious model code can exploit training-time execution permissions in the main Llama-3.2-3B LoRA setting to memorize synthetic secrets, and model code is a key supply-chain boundary | Cross-model/architecture generalization, bypassing DP-SGD/ONION, and 98%+ real-secret theft all unreliable; the original PDF still has placeholders and abstract conflicts with body |
| Decoupled Smart Contract Audits (`2606.03128`) | Task decoupling reduces single-stage VRAM and is easier to optimize than same-base unified prompts; end-to-end attrition should be explicitly reported | 98.25% does not represent professional audit-report success rate, real four-stage all-correct only 7/44; benign, split, LLM judge, and no artifacts limit unknown-project extrapolation |
| MOTIF (`2601.01673`) | Tool augmentation, type constraints, and linter feedback improve Objective-C signature recovery, suitable for reverse Agents | 70%/81% split conflict, no absolute denominator or contamination audit; 15%→86% uses Claude, cannot be written as self-developed Qwen3-8B score or reproducible benchmark |
| Implicit Patterns (`2603.19138`) | Long-horizon binary Agent trajectories reveal observable pruning, persistence, backtracking, and prior-guided focus, suitable as telemetry | 83.5%–97.6% prevalence, cross-model stability, token-level implicit, and security effects all not independently validated; artifacts currently inaccessible |
| Human-Written vs. AI-Generated Code (`2508.21634`) | 507,044 paired tasks show human and three-class AI code differ significantly in static defects, security-rule hits, and complexity distribution; public data suits recomputing descriptive results | Semgrep rule hit is not a real vulnerability; cannot rely on AI code having higher true vulnerability rate, Java DeepSeek risk exactly 6.51× human, or that ratio universally holding across models |
| SCRUTINEER (`2511.11411`) | Structured component knowledge, RAG candidates, similarity, and snapshot-consistency gate identify logic-level usage violations on 51 historical events, and the gate discards 72.91% raw candidates | 13 zero-day, 9 CVE, exact generalization, and universal baseline superiority cannot be independently relied on; CVE IDs missing and paper artifact page expired |
| MAVUL (`2510.00317`) | Architect role, caller/callee tools, and multi-round interaction raise P-C to 35/200 on the given vulnerable/fixed pair semantic matching task | 600% does not represent high detection capability; judge reads ground truth, 17.5% cannot be interpreted as project-level recall, precision, or patched-code security rate |
| SPLVD (`2511.09212`) | Self-paced selector brings ~1.4–3.5pp F1 gain on the same-backbone comparison across three function-level datasets | 89.2% cannot extrapolate to unknown projects; hard samples ≠ wrong labels, OpenHarmony small-sample advantage, cross-project generalization, and threshold interpretation all unreliable |
| LibScan (`2604.00657`) | LLM semantic candidates and static pattern checker are reasonably complementary, and the eight-class library-misuse taxonomy is an independent reproduction starting point | 85.15% independent-test score, 16pp advantage, and fusion efficacy unreliable; suspected label leakage and GPTScan P/R/F1 mathematically not closed |
| Cross-Language CodeBERT (`2508.11710`) | The only recomputable fact is an unexplained 1,990-sample matrix corresponding to ~97.24% accuracy | 98.05% precision, 97.68% F1, cross-language generalization, superior to SAST, CodeLlama/GraphCodeBERT, and GPT-4 verifier effectiveness all unreliable |
| SecCodeBench-V2 (`2602.15485`) | 98 industrial-source function-level secure generation/repair tasks, five languages, 90 dual-deterministic test cases, Docker, and per-round raw results are indeed public | Cannot rely on the paper proving a model is safer, nor treat designated-function Pass@1 as open-ended repository audit capability; 22/14 CWE, severity, and live artifact versions need separation |
| TPL-Benchmark (`2509.04091`) | Existing Android TPL tools remain clearly fragile on R8, version distinction, and scale cost, and the public package proves the author did run large-scale tools | Cannot extrapolate candidate-set F1 containing ground truth to the open world, nor treat 3,309 TPL-CVE/8,115 App-CVE mappings as confirmed exploitable vulnerabilities |
| LLMs Versus SAST (`2508.04448`) | Under a protocol of 10 C# projects, 63 known vulnerabilities and relaxed LLM location, three LLMs' macro-average F1 exceeds three SAST tools | Cannot rely on LLMs universally beating static analysis, exact localization, or proven real-repo low false positives; single-run and public repo lacks benchmark/raw outputs |
| CoTDeceptor (`2512.21250`) | Adaptive multi-round, lineage-based semantic obfuscation is an attack surface audit Agents must include in red-teaming | Cannot rely on 500 samples all semantically preserved, 14/15 as high sample-level ASR, 1--12 rollout as cost ceiling, or 9-positive fine-tuning gain being statistically stable |
| SecureRAG-RTL (`2603.05689`) | On 14 controlled vulnerable RTL designs, summary + keyword retrieval and CWE context improve positive-hit counts for several models | Cannot rely on precision, FPR, open world, real RTL root-cause localization, or low-cost conclusions; all-positive, GPT-5 same-source construction and no public artifacts |

| A.S.E (`2508.18106`) | repository-level completion, quality/security/stability dimensions, and CVE-customized rules expose the decoupling of code models' high-quality/high-stability and low security | `v_after < v_before` only proves SAST alert decline, not root-cause fixing; `:latest` image, 52.79/63.01, ranking error, and no 26-model raw output limit the exact leaderboard |

| MOSAIC-Bench (`2605.03952`) | Cross-ticket state, final-state oracle, session comparison, and reviewer protocol almost directly become an audit-Agent evaluation skeleton | Can rely on the directional conclusion that compositional staging significantly bypasses single-prompt defense; exact ASR, reviewer mean, and FP need recomputation per artifacts |
| R+R (`2605.31135`) | Known-misuse negative constraints, secure examples, model-aware retrieval, and post-generation verifier directly transferable | Can rely on relative trends under the current 12-function/13-misuse taxonomy; cannot generalize to unknown-vulnerability discovery or real-repo security rate |
| Solidity Semantic Clones (`2604.26526`) | Comment/LLM summaries can recall behaviorally similar but differently implemented functions, suitable as a front-end for vulnerability-pattern transfer and implementation-substitution search | Can rely on data-scale original page, method flow, and some human observations; cannot rely on overall recall, Top-20 clone count, or LLM semantic-equivalence conclusions |
| MARD (`2604.25264`) | macro screening, candidate funnel, on-demand Soot/FlowDroid forensics, and verifier layering directly transferable to an audit Agent | Can rely on architecture description and arithmetic in the 124-entry ledger; cannot extrapolate to claimed complete data scale, temporal generalization, and cost |
| Refusal Evaluation Review (`2605.20351`) | Can directly borrow prompt source, judge, execution oracle, license, and version fields; cannot directly reuse its corpus count and coverage statistics | Can rely on its directional judgment that "existing evaluation constructs are highly heterogeneous"; cannot rely on fully-open, independent corpus count, or quantitative coverage conclusions |
| Android Obfuscation Detection (`2606.14233`) | Filtering, sampling, duplicated reasoning, evidence aggregation, and threshold curves inform mobile audit Agents | Can rely on architecture description and some public aggregate results; cannot rely on paper Table 3, complete data scale, or RQ4 F1 |
| PYPILINE (`2606.19063`) | "static API evidence + dedicated knowledge base + Agent report" directly informs package supply-chain audit Agents; must redo data isolation and independent validation | Can rely on architecture description and some in-table arithmetic; cannot rely on 98.1% F1, 99.6% recall, 0.6-second latency, or real generalization conclusions |
| FusionVul (`2606.08553`) | sequence/CPG dual-path evidence and branch ablation borrowable, but must rewrite token-node interaction, input-dependency gating, and project-level evaluation | Can rely on the limited fusion gain reported on the author's random split; cannot rely on cross-attention, sample-aware, fine-grained alignment, or cross-project generalization narrative |

## 2. Scoring Methodology

"Content value" is neither citation count nor "the bigger the number in the abstract the higher the score." The manual judgment focuses on:

1. Whether the problem is important, and whether the paper truly answers the problem.
2. Whether the experimental target is close to a real project, rather than only testing isolated functions or synthetic samples.
3. Whether there is reliable ground truth, strong baselines, ablations, repeated runs, statistical uncertainty, and failure analysis.
4. Whether the code, data, and the method described in the paper are consistent, and whether a third party can re-verify the core conclusions.
5. Whether the conclusion is restrained, distinguishing between "cases prove it is feasible" and "scale experiments prove it is generally effective."

"Value to the project" defaults to the project being a repository-level AI security code audit Agent, focusing on:

- Repository navigation driven by CPG, call graph, control flow, and data flow;
- Project-specific source, sink, sanitizer, guard, and lifecycle protocols;
- Co-accumulation of supporting evidence and counter-evidence;
- Security oracles such as compilation, testing, ASAN, and differential execution;
- Budgets for tokens, time, tool calls, and manual review;
- Output that is locatable, explainable, re-verifiable, and does not automatically inflate vulnerabilities.

## 3. First Batch of Per-Paper In-Depth Reviews

> The full per-paper reviews for the second batch — GONDAR, Sifting the Noise, CVE-Factory, MemRepair, Antaeus, and OpenAnt — are in [`深度审稿_第二批_基准修复逻辑漏洞与误报治理.md`](./深度审稿_第二批_基准修复逻辑漏洞与误报治理.md).

## 1. 2601.19239: LLM-based Vulnerability Detection at Project Scale

**Content value: A, 8.7/10. The most solid of the first batch.**  
**Project value: 9.7/10. Best suited as a template for project motivation, experimental protocol, and failure analysis.**

### What it truly solves

This paper does not propose yet another "smarter Agent"; instead, it places existing project-level LLM vulnerability detectors under conditions close to real deployment and examines how many known vulnerabilities they can actually find, how many false positives they generate on new projects, why those false positives arise, and how many tokens and how much time they cost.

This kind of negative empirical study is more valuable than yet another system paper that only improves F1 by a few points on function-level data, because it directly tests the promotional narrative that "LLMs are already able to audit repositories unattended."

### Strong experimental points

- Actually runs **5 project-level LLM vulnerability detectors** and **2 traditional static analyzers**, not just compares prompts.
- The known-vulnerability benchmark includes **222 real vulnerabilities**, covering C/C++, Java, and 8 CWE classes.
- The real-deployment portion covers **24 active open-source projects**, including Linux, Jenkins, Keycloak, Spark, Vim, ImageMagick, and others.
- Two authors independently inspected **385 sampled alerts**, investing more than **150 person-hours**, and resolved disagreements through discussion.
- Simultaneously measures recall, sampled false-discovery rate, false-positive root causes, tokens, and end-to-end runtime.
- Builds an actionable taxonomy for the 363 false positives, rather than loosely attributing them to "LLM hallucination."
- The paper publicly states that evaluation scripts, prompts, taxonomy labels, and detailed statistics are released for external inspection.

### The most important numbers

- On known vulnerabilities, LLM methods' average recall: C/C++ **21.09% (27/128)**, Java **33.82% (69/204)**.
- In real projects, even the best-performing tool has an average SFDR of **85.3%**.
- RepoAudit's average SFDR on the real-project sample is **97.0%**; IRIS's is **94.4%**.
- Shallow or missing project-level data-flow reasoning accounts for **37.47% (136/363)** of false positives.
- Incorrect source/sink identification accounts for **19.00% (69/363)** of false positives.
- These two categories together contribute more than half of the false positives, showing that what most urgently needs fixing is not prompt wording but program-semantic modeling.
- RepoAudit's highest single-project consumption exceeds **225 million input tokens** and **38.078 million output tokens**.
- LLMDFA's longest single project reaches **4,638 minutes**; its average project time is about **2,000 minutes**.

### Why these conclusions are credible

It uses two complementary settings:

- The known-vulnerability set has ground truth, suited for measuring recall;
- The newest real projects lack complete ground truth, but are suited for observing alert floods, sampled false positives, and actual cost.

The paper does not use one dataset to pretend to answer all questions at once, nor does it report only the prettiest F1. More importantly, the failure taxonomy and the numerical results support each other: low recall and high false positives can both be traced back to source/sink, cross-procedure paths, guard/sanitizer, build conditions, and language semantics, rather than stopping at "the model is not big enough."

### Main limitations

1. **Asymmetric tool support.** Different tools support different CWEs, languages, models, and tool permissions, so the horizontal ranking is not a fully fair same-condition competition.
2. **The real-project sample is small.** Each tool inspects at most 10 alerts per project, so SFDR has large sampling error, and the paper does not adequately report confidence intervals for each estimate.
3. **Real projects lack complete ground truth.** Therefore one can estimate sampled false positives but cannot simultaneously obtain the real projects' complete recall.
4. **The known-vulnerability set mainly tests positives.** Because the non-vulnerability space is not fully annotated, this part reports only recall, without a complete precision/F1.
5. **The technology becomes outdated quickly.** Specific models and API versions change, but the failure mechanisms are more stable than model rankings.
6. **There is one textual inconsistency.** The body and abstract give Java recall 33.82%, but Finding 1 writes 32.70%; this should be treated as an editorial error.
7. **Limited language coverage.** The conclusions mainly come from C/C++ and Java and cannot be directly generalized to Rust, Go, TypeScript, and large multilingual repositories.

### Do the limitations undermine the core conclusion

**No.** They reduce the credibility of "which tool is absolutely the best" and limit extrapolation of specific percentages; but they do not overturn the core finding that "current project-level detection is low-recall, high-false-positive, and high-cost, and is mainly constrained by project semantics and cross-procedure analysis."

### Direct implications for the project

- Learn a project's own allocator/free, source/sink, sanitizer, auth guard, and resource protocols before making vulnerability judgments.
- Do not allow high-confidence vulnerability claims based only on a single function or a fixed three-layer call chain.
- Turn "whether a guard exists on the dominating path," "whether build macros are mutually exclusive," and "whether the call target belongs to the current platform" into mandatory evidence items.
- Reports must include both supporting evidence and counter-evidence that could contradict the conclusion.
- Evaluation must include real-project false positives, manual review burden, and cost, not just function-level F1.
- Default positioning is an audit prioritization tool, not a judge that automatically publishes vulnerabilities.

### Final judgment

If in the future you publish your own repository-level audit Agent, this is the work you must squarely confront. The paper must prove: how your method reduces the 85%–97% false positives it found, how it handles project-specific APIs, how it fills in cross-procedure evidence, and how much those improvements cost.

---

## 2. 2603.13384: VulnAgent-R2

**Method content value: A, about 9.0/10.**  
**Current overall content value: A-/B+, 7.8/10.**  
**Project value: 9.6/10. The most worth borrowing method blueprint, but the experiments must be independently reproduced.**

### What is truly valuable about it

VulnAgent-R2 defines vulnerability detection as "budget-constrained evidence accumulation and calibration," rather than chat-style multi-Agent voting. This abstraction is correct, and is closer to the essence of security auditing than a simple router plus several expert Agents.

Its main modules form a relatively complete system:

- Typed repository graph and initial risk triage;
- Selecting caller, callee, data-flow neighbors, validators, tests, config, and historical patches under a token budget;
- Role specialization such as Data-flow, Control/state, and Memory-safety;
- A Sceptic Agent that actively seeks guards, unreachable paths, and security counterexamples;
- CER's counterfactual perturbations over evidence, such as masking, injecting sanitizers, swapping callers, and replacing historical fix versions;
- VPS converts a vulnerability claim into a verification plan with preconditions, stimulus inputs, oracle, adapter, and timeout;
- Explicitly marking build failure, dependency missing, timeout, flaky, and oracle unavailable as inconclusive, not safe;
- Using confidence calibration and a cost-risk Pareto scheduler to decide whether the next step is worth more token and tool time.

### Key numbers reported by the paper

- Devign F1/AUROC: **0.798/0.895**.
- Big-Vul: **0.739/0.871**.
- DiverseVul: **0.700/0.842**.
- PrimeVul: **0.385/0.781**.
- JITVul: F1 **0.606**, Top-1 **0.529**, Top-3 **0.742**.
- Compared with always-full multi-Agent, average tokens fall from **15,240** to **9,410**, a reduction of **38.3%**; F1 drops slightly from .388 to .385.
- Compared with VulnAgent-X, PrimeVul F1 improves **0.038**, 95% CI **[0.020, 0.055]**, Holm-corrected **p=.009**.
- VPS triggers on **21.6%** of samples; after triggering, **68.9%** form a runnable test or harness, **35.7%** produce positive executable evidence, and **25.8%** are clearly inconclusive.
- A manual audit of 120 verifier traces claims a classification correctness of **93.3%**.

### Why the abalation is instructive

On PrimeVul:

- Full system F1 **.385**;
- single-agent only **.238**;
- removing the graph-context optimizer drops to **.337**;
- removing CER drops to **.359**;
- removing Sceptic drops to **.352**;
- removing VPS drops to **.346**;
- removing calibration still leaves F1 .369, but ECE degrades from .061 to **.148**;
- removing budget scheduling raises F1 slightly to .388, but tokens rise from 9,410 to **15,240**.

If the results are reproducible, this set of ablations nicely shows that the different modules respectively improve detection, calibration, verifiability, and cost, rather than all modules existing just to stack F1.

### The most critical credibility problem: the paper and the public repo do not match

This is an additional downgrade item that must be added after this close read.

1. arXiv v1 (2026-03-11) is still titled **VulnAgent-X**, the experimental protocol is relatively brief, and PrimeVul F1 is **.316**.
2. v2/v3 were substantially rewritten into **VulnAgent-R2** between 2026-06-02 and 06-03, adding CER, VPS, calibration, statistical tests, many new datasets, and new results.
3. v3 claims the artifacts include `prompts/*.json`, `agent protocol.yaml`, `tool policy.yaml`, split IDs, raw predictions, verifier traces, and plotting scripts.
4. The public repository given by the paper is still named **VulnAgent-X Research Prototype**, and its sole initial commit date is the same day as v1.
5. The README explicitly says the workflow uses `verification(stub)` / "safe placeholder."
6. The repository does not contain artifacts corresponding to CER, VPS, R2, the paper's Table 10 ablations, raw predictions, or verifier traces.
7. The repo's 9 unit tests pass, which shows the old prototype at least runs; but this cannot reproduce v3's R2 results.
8. The public verification implementation mainly runs Python compilation, Semgrep, and optional pytest, and is not equivalent to the paper's described VPS that synthesizes per-CWE ASAN/boundary/differential oracles.

This is not a small README lag; rather, the core new methods and the core experimental artifacts do not yet have corresponding public implementations. It directly affects the verifiability of the numerical results, ablations, and statistical significance.

### Other experimental risks

- The system is heavy: CodeT5+ triage, graph attention, evidence scorer, multiple Agents, CER, dynamic verifier, calibrator, and scheduler all participate, making reproduction difficult.
- The CER scorer is trained on 6,420 evidence tuples; although the paper claims repository isolation and exclusion of test CVEs, the labels, features, and training process require original artifacts to audit.
- Only methods designed to be verifiable can use the verifier; the paper adds Verifier-RAG diagnostics to mitigate fairness, but different Agents' actual tool opportunities are still not exactly equal.
- The shared index averaging 9.4 minutes/repository is excluded from online cost. That is reasonable for continuous auditing, but the total cost of the first scan cannot be ignored.
- PrimeVul F1 .385 is still very low; when a high-precision threshold raises precision to .561, recall falls to only .214.
- Dynamic verification suits memory, integer, and parser vulnerabilities, but for Python/JavaScript framework permission issues it still lacks runtime mocks.

### Do the limitations undermine the core conclusion

It needs to be answered at two levels:

- **The method direction is not shaken.** Counterexample, executable verification, the inconclusive state, calibration, and budget scheduling are all very worth adopting.
- **All precise experimental conclusions are shaken.** Until the R2 code, split, raw predictions, and verifier traces are consistent and public, the .385 F1, p=.009, or 38.3% token savings cannot be written as independently established facts.

### Direct implications for the project

- Represent each vulnerability conclusion as a structured evidence tuple, rather than a passage of natural language.
- Set up a dedicated counterexample Agent that is forced to seek sanitizers, guards, unreachable paths, platform mutual exclusion, and security historical patches.
- Dynamic verification failure must be `inconclusive`, and must never be converted to `safe`.
- The verifier accepts only parseable symbols and observable oracles, avoiding the LLM generating plausible-looking but non-executable test plans.
- Confidence must be calibrated on a validation set, not directly using the LLM's self-reported confidence.
- Allocate tokens by risk and expected information gain; low-risk negatives exit early, and high-risk disputed items get increased evidence budgets.

### Final judgment

It is still one of the most suitable of the current 186 papers for inspiring a "pure audit Agent reasoning architecture"; if discussing a complete discover—validate platform, Antiproof's system boundary is more complete. When citing R2, it should be written as:

> "VulnAgent-R2 proposes an evidence-calibration architecture worth validating."

Rather than:

> "VulnAgent-R2 has been proven by public artifacts to reach .385 F1 and significantly outperform baselines."

After independent reproduction is completed, the content score can rise from 7.8 to about 8.7–9.0; if it cannot be reproduced, it should be lowered further.

---

## 3. 2606.04739: Revisiting Vul-RAG

**Content value: B+, 7.7/10.**  
**Project value: 8.3/10. The knowledge representation is worth borrowing; the detection performance should not be overestimated.**

### First distinguish the "original method" from "this paper"

Within the recent one-year window is the independent reproduction paper **Revisiting Vul-RAG**. The original method it re-verifies, Vul-RAG, has been accepted by TOSEM; its core idea is knowledge-level RAG.

So the evaluation here merges two parts:

- The methodological innovation of the original Vul-RAG;
- 2606.04739's examination of the reproducibility of the original result and its copyability across models.

### The most valuable innovation of the original method

Vul-RAG does not directly retrieve similar code, but extracts three-dimensional knowledge from historical CVEs and fix pairs:

1. **Functional Semantics**: function purpose and detailed behavior;
2. **Vulnerability Causes**: preconditions, trigger conditions, and the specific behavior leading to the vulnerability;
3. **Fixing Solutions**: the repair strategy adopted by the patch.

In the online stage it first extracts the functional semantics of the code under audit, retrieves top-N knowledge using BM25 and re-ranking, then checks each item one by one: whether the code matches the historical vulnerability cause, and whether the corresponding fix has already been implemented.

This representation is more valuable than "similar code snippets + CWE description," because it tries to abstract away variable names, function names, and specific types while preserving the causal structure.

### Strong points of the reproduction experiment

- Completed by an independent team, not the original authors self-reproducing.
- Rebuilds the original API pipeline into HuggingFace local open-weight inference.
- Uses the original data, prompts, knowledge base, and default hyperparameters, isolating as much as possible the single variable of "only swapping models."
- Qwen2.5-Coder-32B runs 3 times and reports mean and standard deviation.
- Releases code, data, per-model outputs, per-CWE metrics, and analysis scripts.
- Does not hide failures: DeepSeek-Coder-V2, even using 8 H100 80GB GPUs, still has 21/586 pairs incomplete due to OOM.
- Explicitly discusses threats such as data contamination, model-selection bias, hardware threshold, and strict output format.

### Key numbers

- The dataset totals **2,903 Linux kernel vulnerability/fix function pairs**, 586 of which are the PairVul test set, covering 10 CWEs.
- The original paper's best GPT-4o: PairAcc **.32**, balanced recall **.58**, balanced precision **.63**.
- Random binary classification on pairwise accuracy is **.25**.
- Qwen2.5-Coder-32B originally reported PairAcc .26; the reproduction is still **.26 ± .01**, basically successful.
- DeepSeek-Coder-V2 originally reported .30; the reproduction is only **.22**, and only **565/586** pairs complete.
- The new Qwen3-Coder-30B only reaches **.28**.
- The reasoning models QwQ-32B and DeepSeek-R1-32B also peak at only **.29**.
- DeepSeek-R1-8B also reaches **.29**, showing parameter scale is not the main bottleneck.
- QwQ-32B's median output rises from 540 to **1,385 tokens (2.6×)**, but buys only a very small performance change.

### Why this paper has value

It does not force a positive conclusion under the banner of "newer models are stronger"; instead it proves:

- The Qwen result can be basically reproduced;
- The DeepSeek result fails to be reproduced;
- Swapping in newer, larger, or explicitly reasoning models does not break through;
- PairAcc peaks at .29, only 4 percentage points above random .25.

This kind of result guides the project better than "yet another 2% improvement": the problem may be stuck in knowledge quality, retrieval matching, and task definition, rather than the model not being big enough.

### Main limitations

1. **The reproduction only covers the online stage.** To control variables, it reuses the old knowledge base generated by GPT-3.5-turbo-0125, without regenerating knowledge or doing a knowledge-quality ablation.
2. **Narrow scope.** It only covers Linux kernel, C functions, and 10 CWEs, still far from multilingual repository-level auditing.
3. **The function-pair task is not project auditing.** The vulnerable and patch functions have already been cut out, lacking cross-file localization, calling context, and alert search space.
4. **Random train/test split.** Compared with isolating by time or project, this may more easily share project style and historical patterns.
5. **Data contamination cannot be ruled out.** PairVul was public in 2024, and several tested models postdate the data release.
6. **Format-selection bias.** Models that cannot stably output `<result>YES/NO</result>` are excluded, so part of the measured capability may be instruction following.
7. **The output lacks verifiable evidence.** It ultimately remains mostly a binary judgment, making it hard for developers to audit the conclusion.
8. **.30 is not a theoretical ceiling.** It is only an empirical plateau under the current fixed knowledge base, retrieval pipeline, dataset, and model.

### Do the limitations undermine the core conclusion

**They do not overturn the reproduction paper's core observation, but they limit extrapolation.** One can believe "on the existing Vul-RAG configuration, merely swapping in a larger model gives no clear breakthrough"; one cannot claim "the limit of all knowledge RAG is .30."

### Direct implications for the project

- Model historical vulnerability knowledge as `precondition -> trigger -> dangerous behavior -> fix/guard`.
- Simultaneously use functional semantics, call/data-flow structure, and CWE priors when retrieving, avoiding sole reliance on BM25 text similarity.
- Perform quality scoring, versioning, and counterexample maintenance on the knowledge base itself; wrong knowledge forms a system upper bound.
- Treat "whether a fix pattern already exists" as counter-evidence, rather than only matching the vulnerability cause.
- Do not spend the whole budget on a bigger model; prioritize improving knowledge extraction, retrieval, and project context.
- Output must cite specific paths, data flows, guards, and historical patches, to supplement the original method's binary black-box result.

### Final judgment

Vul-RAG's core is not the three words "RAG," but "abstracting patch differences into reusable vulnerability causal knowledge." This idea has value; but the original detection result is far from production-ready, and should serve as the audit Agent's experience-memory module rather than the primary judge.

---

## 4. 2512.16538: A Systematic Study of Code Obfuscation Against LLM-based Vulnerability Detection

**Content value: B, 6.8/10.**  
**Project value: 8.2/10. Suited for development into a robustness-testing chapter, but the experiments cannot be copied as-is.**

### What is truly valuable about it

This paper focuses on a frequently overlooked problem: if the same vulnerability undergoes semantics-preserving transformations of variable names, layout, data-flow expression, or control-flow structure, is the LLM's conclusion stable?

This direction is important. Real attackers do not write vulnerabilities in textbook style, and project code naturally contains macros, templates, generated code, inline assembly, state machines, and complex control flow. Measuring accuracy only on clean functions easily overestimates the model.

### Where the coverage is strong

- Builds a taxonomy of 3 major categories, 11 subcategories, and 19 specific obfuscation techniques.
- Covers four languages: Solidity, C, C++, Python.
- The raw data contains SmartBugs 128, PrimeVul 143, ReposVul C++ 84, ReposVul Python 210 files, totaling **565 vulnerability samples**.
- Evaluates **15 LLMs**, covering four families: Qwen, LLaMA, DeepSeek, OpenAI.
- Also tests two coding Agents: GitHub Copilot and Codex.
- Tries not only to answer "does a vulnerability exist" but to distinguish vulnerability type and location.
- Finds that obfuscation causes both downgrades and upgrades, indicating model judgments depend heavily on surface cues.

### Key findings

- The paper claims virtualization obfuscation can reach about **80%** attack success rate on C/C++.
- Mixed programming-language obfuscation is about **75%**.
- When doing only binary existence evaluation, the detection success rate is on average about **40%** higher than strict type evaluation.
- This shows models can often vaguely say "there may be a vulnerability," but cannot reliably identify the correct type and location.
- Large and reasoning models are usually stronger on clean code, but some families show even clearer downgrades after obfuscation.
- Agents are not immune either; both the underlying model and the Agent toolchain may introduce fragility.

### The most serious problem: insufficient evidence of semantic equivalence

The paper uses GPT-4o to generate obfuscated code and claims the authors "manually verified each obfuscated sample and fixed errors." But:

- It does not clearly report how many samples failed on first generation, how many were corrected, and how many were finally discarded;
- It does not report compile pass rate, test pass rate, or vulnerability retention rate per language;
- It does not explain how it confirmed the target vulnerability remains triggerable after obfuscation, rather than merely that the code is roughly readable or compilable;
- 565 samples multiplied by many combinations form thousands of variants, so a single sentence "manually verified one by one" is insufficient to support a repeatable semantic-equivalence guarantee;
- The Appendix prompt merely asks the model to "preserve functionality, be compilable, and runnable," and a requirement itself is not verification.

If the transformation fixes the vulnerability, changes a reachable path, breaks the build, or introduces a new vulnerability, then the detection rise or fall cannot be entirely attributed to robustness.

### Other experimental problems

1. **Inconsistent reporting of combination counts.** The body says it constructs 10 obfuscation combos, but the listed content actually exceeds 10; the results figure also shows D8, and it ultimately looks like 12 groups, affecting the experiment's re-verifiability.
2. **Contains only vulnerability samples.** The main metric observes whether the target vulnerability is still detected, and cannot adequately measure whether obfuscation induces new false positives.
3. **Score 4 is over-permissive.** As long as the report contains the real vulnerability, even if it simultaneously hallucinates multiple wrong vulnerabilities, it counts as a success.
4. **The definition of "location correct" is inadequate.** The formal scoring mainly revolves around the vulnerability-type set; how location is judged by an LLM-as-a-judge has no sufficiently transparent rules.
5. **The LLM-as-a-judge validation scale is small.** It only claims 100% agreement on 100 curated cases, without reporting a fuller sample, confusion matrix, or human-labeler agreement.
6. **Lacks confidence intervals and significance.** Many model vs. transformation comparisons are prone to chance rankings, but the paper mainly gives point estimates and counts.
7. **The Agent experiment has selection bias.** Some extension analyses only take the downgrade top-20, so a small sample cannot support strong generalization conclusions.
8. **The generator is correlated with the tested models.** Transformations generated by GPT-4o may carry its own model preferences, so generating-distribution coupling exists when re-testing GPT-4o/OpenAI families.

### Do the limitations undermine the core conclusion

Two levels:

- **The qualitative conclusion still holds:** LLM vulnerability detection is unstable under syntax, layout, data-flow, and control-flow transformations, and strict type/location evaluation exposes problems better than binary judgment.
- **The precise numbers cannot be fully established:** 80%, 75%, and the model-family robustness ranking need executable semantic verification and statistical re-checking before being strongly cited.

### Direct implications for the project

- Build metamorphic testing: generate multiple semantically-preserving versions of the same vulnerability and require model conclusions to be consistent.
- Each variant must pass the three-layer check of `compile + regression tests + exploit/PoC oracle`.
- Simultaneously test vulnerability samples and safe samples, measuring downgrades, upgrades, new false positives, and confidence drift.
- Keep variable renaming, dead code, equivalent condition rewriting, state-machine transformation, macro expansion, and cross-function splitting as the ongoing evaluation set.
- For Agents, record tool-call changes: does obfuscation affect LLM judgment, or CPG, parser, or retrieval?
- Strict scoring should require CWE, location, source, sink, guard, and reachable path all to be correct simultaneously.

### Final judgment

This is a good paper-experiment direction, but not yet a fully solid robustness benchmark. You can upgrade it into your own strong experimental contribution: the key additions are automated semantic equivalence, vulnerability-retention oracles, safe samples, and confidence intervals.

---

## 5. 2603.24837: codebadger / Joern + CPG

**Academic value: B-/C+, 6.4/10.**  
**Engineering value: A, 9.0/10; considering only the engineering architecture it can reach 9.4/10.**

### What is truly valuable about it

The core of codebadger is not "an LLM found a vulnerability," but wrapping Joern's Code Property Graph capability into high-level tools that LLMs can stably call.

It avoids having the Agent write complex CPGQL on the fly, instead providing:

- Call graph, method and parameter browsing;
- CFG, type definitions, and bounds checks;
- source, sink, taint flow;
- forward/backward program slicing;
- variable flow and pointer aliasing;
- UAF, double free, null dereference, heap/stack overflow;
- integer overflow, format string, TOCTOU, and command injection;
- preserving a raw CPGQL escape hatch when necessary.

This is precisely the tool boundary a repository-level audit Agent needs: the LLM decides the analysis target, and the static analyzer reliably computes graph relations.

### Why the engineering implementation is worth borrowing

The current public repository is not a few-dozen-line demo:

- The MCP server and the Joern process are separated;
- Each CPG runs in its own JVM and locks queries on the same CPG;
- Docker isolates Joern;
- Redis/Postgres manage state, tasks, and caches;
- CPGs are cached by source hash and language;
- Supports asynchronous generation, polling, sleeping, reactivation, and resource reclamation;
- Provides memory budget, RSS eviction, idle TTL, timeout, and concurrency config;
- The current repository has about 58 source files and 51 test files, and has already become a continuously maintained beta project.

These contents are far more valuable to a real project than the three case numbers in the paper.

### Evidence from the three cases

#### GGML large-repository browsing

- 1,151 files, about 246k LOC, more than 8,000 methods.
- The Agent uses summary, source/sink, taint, and slice for directed navigation, rather than stuffing the whole repository into context.
- Proves the tool interface can bypass the context window, but does not quantify how many tokens or seconds it saves, or how many targets it misses.

#### libtiff vulnerability analysis

- 828 files, about 127k LOC.
- The Agent traces `col_offset` crossing procedures into `gtStripContig`'s buffer arithmetic.
- Generates a PoC and uses ASAN to obtain a heap-buffer-overflow at the predicted location.
- Re-tests on the fixed master; the PoC is rejected by the newly added bounds check.

The dynamic evidence is strong, showing this specific case is not pure natural-language hallucination.

But it should be carefully described as "independent rediscovery/validation on a given vulnerable commit": the corresponding fix commit existed on 2025-06-25, while the paper was published in 2026. The paper proving the model's cutoff predates the fix does not prove the authors were unaware of the fix when selecting the case, and cannot automatically equal a forward-looking zero-day discovery experiment.

#### libxml2 CVE-2025-6021 fix

- 1,574 files, about 335k LOC.
- The Agent obtained the original issue, PoC, and ASAN output, then used call relations, data dependency, and slicing to generate a patch.
- The paper claims the first round of no-human-intervention generated patch is close to the maintainer's actual fix.

This is a valuable fix case, but the task already provided the vulnerability-location clue, PoC, and dynamic error, so its difficulty is clearly lower than autonomously discovering, localizing, validating, and fixing from an unknown repository.

### Why the academic experiment only gets 6.4

1. **Only three qualitative cases.** No scale vulnerability benchmark, real-project alert set, or safe negative set.
2. **No baseline.** It does not compare pure RAG, direct file reading, native Joern, CodeQL, other CPG tools, or an Agent without codebadger.
3. **Only one primary model/orchestration combination.** The cases use Claude Sonnet 4.5 + GitHub Copilot Agent, which cannot prove cross-model stability.
4. **No repeat trials.** "first attempt" is a single success, not a success rate.
5. **No false positives or recall.** One ASAN success case cannot be extrapolated into overall low false positives or high vulnerability coverage.
6. **No cost data.** Lacks CPG build time, query latency, token savings, memory, and end-to-end audit cost.
7. **Case-selection information may leak.** The libtiff task directly specifies the file and "buffer underflow," and libxml2 even gives the issue, PoC, and ASAN, so it cannot represent open-ended auditing.
8. **Inherits Joern limitations.** Large repositories have high memory cost, and complex builds, dynamic languages, reflection, macros, and concurrency vulnerabilities may still be under-analyzed.

### Do the limitations undermine the core conclusion

**Engineering feasibility is not shaken, but generalization is.** One can believe "this MCP + CPG tool layer works, and can support cross-procedure analysis and dynamic validation in specific cases"; one cannot believe "it is overall superior to other methods" or "already proven suitable for large-scale automated vulnerability discovery."

### Direct implications for the project

- High-level tools should return structured nodes, edges, paths, and provenance, and not let the LLM directly concatenate arbitrary graph queries.
- Separate and cache CPG generation from online auditing, and account first-time cost and incremental cost separately.
- The tool layer must have query timeout, result caps, per-CPG locks, memory budget, and container isolation.
- Use `get_program_slice`, `find_taint_flows`, and `find_bounds_checks` as the Agent's basic evidence actions.
- Keep raw query as a restricted escape hatch, and provide syntax help and retry budget for failed queries.
- In the paper experiments, add benchmarks, a no-codebadger baseline, multiple-model repeated runs, and real-project false positives.

### Final judgment

If asked "which paper's engineering ideas are most worth copying," codebadger is first. The most reasonable way to use it is not as an already-proven-effective complete detector, but as your audit Agent's static-semantic tool base.

---

## 6. 2308.10345: Can Large Language Models Find And Fix Vulnerable Software?

> This paper was published in 2023 and is outside the recent one-year window; it is included here only as a historical comparison.

**Content value: C-/D+, 3.9/10.**  
**Project value: 4.4/10. Usable for writing development history, not suitable as core experimental evidence.**

### Why it mattered at the time

In 2023, putting GPT-4 and Snyk/Fortify together to discuss vulnerability discovery and automatic repair itself had early-exploration significance. The paper also proposed a closed loop that later became common:

1. The LLM finds the vulnerability;
2. The LLM gives the fix;
3. The fixed code is handed back to the static scanner for re-checking.

This "detect—fix—re-scan" idea is still worth borrowing.

### Main numbers reported by the paper

- Uses 128/129 code samples, covering 8 languages and 33 vulnerability classes; different sections of the paper disagree between 128 and 129 on the sample count.
- Snyk finds about **98/99** issues.
- GPT-4 reports **393** issues, which the paper uses to claim roughly four times Snyk.
- GPT-4 also gives about **398** fix suggestions.
- After GPT-4's rewrite, Snyk alerts fall from **98 to 10**, which the paper says is a reduction of about 90%.
- Code line count increases by about **11%**.

### Core methodological errors

#### 1. Treating "number of reports" as "discovery capability"

GPT-4 reporting 393 and Snyk reporting 99 does not mean GPT-4 found four times the real vulnerabilities. Without complete ground truth, more reports could just be more false positives, duplicate descriptions, or counting general code-quality problems as vulnerabilities.

#### 2. Treating "can give fix suggestions" as "evidence of low false positives"

The paper thinks every finding has a fix suggestion, so this supports a low false-positive rate. This reasoning does not hold: the LLM can generate plausible-looking fixes for problems that do not exist, and the fix suggestion itself may break functionality or introduce new problems.

#### 3. Using Snyk to verify GPT-4's fixes, but not verifying all of GPT-4's findings

After fixing, Snyk alerts falling from 98 to 10 only shows the rewrite eliminated most of the patterns **Snyk can identify**. It cannot prove how many of the 393 issues GPT-4 reported are real, nor that GPT-4 fixed 90% of the real vulnerabilities.

#### 4. No functional correctness or executable security verification

The paper does not systematically report:

- Whether the rewritten code compiles;
- Whether the original tests pass;
- Whether the sample's functionality is preserved;
- Whether the PoC no longer triggers;
- Whether new vulnerabilities or behavior changes are introduced.

A simple 11% increase in code lines does not mean the patch is high quality.

### Other main limitations

- It only randomly manually checked 60/213 of the earlier GPT-3 results, not a strict re-verification of this paper's GPT-4 393 findings.
- No precision, recall, F1, confidence intervals, repeated runs, or statistical tests.
- Snyk's support differences across C/Go etc. make the raw count comparison unfair.
- The so-called NASA, DoD and other large repositories mainly show Snyk alert counts, without forming a same-ground-truth, same-file, same-rule GPT-4 comparison.
- Most code snippets are only a few hundred tokens, and the paper itself admits it cannot analyze system-level vulnerabilities, dependencies, and library errors.
- Single author, single model version, prompt sensitivity, and API drift are all not systematically controlled.
- The Appendix contains examples that generalize `import os`, ordinary `open()`, etc. into potential vulnerabilities, which actually exposes the high false-positive risk.

### Do the limitations undermine the core conclusion

**Yes.** The paper can prove GPT-4 generates rich vulnerability explanations and code modifications, and can make some of Snyk's alerts disappear; but it cannot prove GPT-4 found four times the real vulnerabilities, has very low false positives, or fixed 90% of the real security defects.

### What can still be kept for the project

- Keep the closed loop of "generate a fix after detection, then re-check with an independent tool."
- Treat "whether it can propose a fix" as evidence to be verified, not as proof of a true positive.
- All fixes must pass compilation, tests, PoC, static scanning, and differential behavior checks.
- The paper can appear in an early stage of related work, and should not carry the core argument of method effectiveness.

### Final judgment

It is suitable as historical material to explain "how early research overestimated LLM vulnerability detection," and forms a sharp contrast with 2601.19239: the former infers capability from alert count, while the latter reveals the real gap through ground truth, manual review, a false-positive taxonomy, and cost.

---

## 4. Turning 329 Papers into a Deployable Project

The most reasonable route is not to replicate any single paper, but to assemble the parts of the thirty-nine batches of papers that are already established or worth independent verification into one evidence chain:

```text
CVE-Factory continuous task factory
  -> Taught by the Flawed only supplies the motivation for analyzer-aware data governance; actually adopt multiple routes of filter/reweight/repair/contrastive pair and keep the original findings
  -> Executable training and evaluation tasks isolated by project, commit, and disclosure time
  -> ARVO rebuilds historical OSS-Fuzz issues into vulnerable/fixed images, PoCs, patch locating, and multi-PoC regression assets
  -> CyberGym-E2E uses S1-S4 to split localization, repair, functional preservation, and hidden-target PoC, with S4 as the end-to-end target-vulnerability gate
  -> SecureVibeBench rebuilds real vulnerability-introducing scenarios, and binds functional tests, PoV, and SAST suspicious items
  -> RustMizan-style compile-first vulnerable/safe pairs, file/function context ladder, and contamination mutation diagnostics
  -> AVIATOR-style generation of clean/vulnerable hard pairs, separating weakness/trigger/exploit labels
  -> CVE-GENIE converts known CVEs into executable build/exploit/verifier/critic reproduction tasks
  -> QLCoder/RuleForge converts confirmed vulnerabilities into versioned CodeQL/Semgrep/Joern/Nuclei detectors
  -> QRS/Argus proposes query/sink hypotheses from CWE, historical CVEs, and project APIs; these only enter the candidate layer and must pass negative examples, patched replay, human root cause, and low-base-rate precision audit
  -> SPECA extracts requirement checklists, actors, trust boundaries, and scope
  -> LogicScan BSL turns permissions, state, amounts, timing, and cross-function conditions into invariant knowledge with provenance
  -> KNOWDIT turns project semantics, historical findings, specs, PoCs, and reflections into an evolvable knowledge graph
  -> CryptoScope-style specialized crypto primitive/constraint/invariant/failure-mode memory, but with the knowledge base strictly isolated from CVE/CTF targets
  -> VulRTex turns issues, PRs, attachments, comments, and historical cases into external hypotheses with provenance and timestamps, which must subsequently be validated by source/dynamic evidence
  -> ALIBI trust separation demotes comments, PRs, issues, tool text, and Agent memory to low-trust inputs, with a code-only lane independently generating judgments
  -> PHOENIX/SBIR maps historical PR/CWEs into anchored semantic contracts across Python, C++, and backend
  -> RepBench compresses AST/CFG/PDG into a typed evidence lane, and does strictly paired ablations against same-scope source code
  -> EvoPoC-style three-layer HKG of Contract Semantics/Failure Mode/Exploit Primitive, with reachable paths and asset states separated
  -> VulInstruct stores general security specs and project/CVE root-cause cases in layers, retaining both raw labels and rationale metrics
  -> VulReaD CWE/security KG provides semantic candidates, and AdaTaint-style project source/sink adaptation provides only candidates and priority, not direct vulnerability adjudication
  -> codebadger/CPG + VulWeaver graph-semantic repair + CPRVUL caller/callee/global compression + Heimdallr graph-community context batching
  -> Antares explore-first locator first narrows file/function candidates within the repository tree, dependencies, and historical patches; benchmark deduplicates by GHSA/task/commit and keeps root-cause unknown
  -> VulTriage-style Control/Knowledge/Semantic typed context lanes; when the precise graph extractor fails it must abstain, and silent heuristic fallback is forbidden
  -> Code Representation / FPR slices the same candidate into source, AST/CPG, and call-chain representations, using cross-representation disagreement as an uncertainty signal, and evaluates the AND/OR/abstain gates separately
  -> CLAWAUDIT compiles the prompt builder, tool dispatcher, skill loader, memory writer, network client, and permission gate into Agent-runtime-specific Semgrep/CodeQL rules
  -> SkillFortify's data plane records declared/inferred capabilities, skill lockfile, ASBOM, dependency graph, and registry provenance; formal guarantees are declared only within explicit concrete semantics
  -> SLYP-style binary entry enumeration, decompilation cross-references, dynamic debugging, and PoC adapters, to cover targets without source
  -> Veritas-style witness-backed slices fix the executable flow, the LLM constructs claims only within the evidence boundary, and the Validator confirms independently
  -> AgenticSCR uses commit/diff as the entry to generate review tasks bound to line numbers, root cause, and a CWE checklist
  -> SecureReviewer-style security review contract and expert-revision flow; negative examples must not use absence-of-comment, and text metrics must not replace evidence correctness
  -> Antiproof scope/attack-surface/detector + GONDAR/OpenAnt candidate funnel
  -> VULSOLVER represents candidates as path, branch, and context constraints over source/transform/guard/sink
  -> DREA/VulnResolver CPCAgent directed evidence supplement + Mastermind strategy Curator reusing task-local success/failure strategies
  -> Vul-RAG/Antaeus project knowledge + MAVM recurrence knowledge
  -> VulnLLM-R specialized security reasoning base + VulnAgent-R2 expert analysis/Sceptic + VulAgent hypothesis pruning/final guard verification + Sifting the Noise cross-file counter-evidence
  -> AEGIS layers clues, typed evidence graphs, Red/Blue supporting and opposing arguments, and independent audit; audit serves only as a restricted veto and cannot override hard program evidence
  -> ReasonVul splits deductive rules, historical induction, and attack abduction into different evidence producers, debates only on conflict, and preserves original positions
  -> Code-Augur writes the implicit assumptions of a secure judgment as assertions, and uses guided fuzzing to counter-prove, revise, or upgrade into a new PoV
  -> FLAMES-style guard/invariant synthesis, then elimination through the four-layer oracle of compile, semantics, functionality, and exploit
  -> LASAA CC/LRE handles unstable alerts, retaining dependent/inconclusive; a valid trigger serves only as strong positive evidence
  -> Heimdallr-style attacker-capability/global-guard/shared-state/economic Verifier
  -> DAGVul causal evidence graph + EntailLLM step-by-step rule verification, rejecting Lucky Hit and unsupported path claims
  -> AutoTrace trigger statement and source-to-sink witness + T2L trace-to-chunk-to-line localization
  -> QuartetFuzz P1-P4 first audits the correctness of the harness/PoC/verifier itself, ruling out harness-induced crashes
  -> SecureForge continuously searches failure prompts and maintains an independent held-out security regression set, disallowing a closed loop where the optimization oracle and the main evaluation oracle share the same source
  -> GRASP security-practice DAG provides only generation constraints with provenance; SCAFFOLD-CEGIS semantic anchor + scope/compile/function/security gate manages secure edits, with the gate separated from the final evaluation oracle
  -> AI Refactoring PR gate performs diff-aware build/test/lint/SAST and finding root-cause attribution for each refactor
  -> SAFuzz-style risk model only schedules the dynamic-validation budget; training/testing must be isolated by problem family with repeated fuzzing
  -> FuzzingBrain V2 Suspicious Point/Direction + global/SP fuzzer, turning static suspicion into dynamic search
  -> Sailor uses CodeQL specs and static analysis to produce candidates, with the LLM only adjusting the path budget and KLEE/ASan producing crashes; KLEECopilot uses stagnation feedback and weight rewriting to cover states random-path does not reach
  -> MULTIVUL-style code/structure/narrative fusion only enters candidate-ranking experiments; first report all-positive, all-negative, majority, and same-backbone single-modality baselines, then do project/CVE/clone isolation
  -> ShadowProbe generates controllable worst-case inputs for complexity candidates, saves multi-scale raw timings, and confirms ACV via robust growth inference
  -> Co-RedTeam code browser/execution/validation is responsible for the post-candidate exploit loop; online memory is frozen per project, version, and evaluation order
  -> KASS typed plan/PoC Generator/inner repair/outer replan, handling attack-hypothesis failure and code errors separately and enforcing target integrity
  -> Bulkhead forms a counterexample-driven repair loop from PoC, candidate patches, compile diagnostics, SPIN counterexample, and legitimate-path harness, with over-restrictive fixes listed separately
  -> Dual-Mode P3/P4 layers vulnerability-family methodology, high-recall candidates, and programmatic evidence rules; the rules themselves must have negative examples and precision audit
  -> VulnSage/REVELIO connects constraint understanding, PoE/PoV generation, reflection repair, and sanitizer replay after the candidate; counting PoE, reproducible exploits, maintainer fixes, and CVE grades
  -> CVE-GENIE/AXE/GONDAR/Antiproof/V2E/SmartPoC/CyberChainBench-style compile, sanitizer, historical-state, PoC, Action-State differential, profit, trigger, impact, permission, or capability oracles
  -> Antiproof Hit/PoV/PoE/Human/Maintainer/CVE grading state machine
  -> CodeCureAgent alert-handling state machine + MemRepair/EvoRepair failure trajectories, quality re-ranking, and versioned experience library; freeze initial memory during evaluation
  -> AgenticRepair builds structure/runtime/history context in parallel, first passing patch-integrity before entering dual-build, tests, and exploit variants
  -> VulnResolver Safety Property instrumentation + Vul4Py/PATCHEVAL exploit/functional paired oracle + selecting the patch after human semantic audit
  -> SecCoderX/Vul-R2 provide only candidate training ideas, with the final reward switched to build/test/exploit/static regression
  -> Strategic Heterogeneous Multi-Agent-style strong-model multi-perspective candidate expansion and small-model independent verifier; local costs such as dual RTX count into the total budget, and the FPR after the verifier must be listed separately
  -> Multi-LLMSecCodeEval-style multi-model only expands candidates, with the external oracle deciding and comparing under equal budgets
  -> ELVul keeps only the competence-routing idea: select models/tools by independent verifier history, and expert inputs and routing labels must be verified per file
  -> VCAO idea schedules token, GPU, static analysis, fuzzing, and human budgets by marginal benefit
  -> SecLens-R-style multi-role dashboard shows underlying metrics and weight sensitivity, keeping missing costs as unknown rather than deleting the denominator
  -> Contextual Bias + Words Speak Louder Than Code-style metadata isolation, direction reversal, and multi-round adaptive red-team replay
  -> Security Calibration-style False Trust, selective refusal, and calibrated SARIF reports
  -> ReVul-CoT does severity triage with strict isolation only after vulnerability confirmation
  -> TP retention, suppression safety gate, and manual review
  -> Hybrid-CASR-style time windows, replay, and retention evaluation; continuous updates accept only real gains with confidence intervals and effect sizes
  -> SecDrift-style neutral/matched/full-context/placebo prompt matrix, interface-drift annotation, CWE-exclusion sensitivity, and manual audit of SAST misses
  -> 2601.19239 + From Lab to Reality-style recall, SFDR, pair, out-of-time, and cost evaluation
  -> CWE-Trace DFI/HDD distinguishes correct detection, paranoid all-report, and skeptical all-non-report, and recomputes the contamination decomposition
```
